This is a digital copy of a book that was preserved for generations on library shelves before it was carefully scanned by Google as part of a project to make the world’s books discoverable online.

It has survived long enough for the copyright to expire and the book to enter the public domain. A public domain book is one that was never subject to copyright or whose legal copyright term has expired. Whether a book is in the public domain may vary from country to country. Public domain books are our gateways to the past, representing a wealth of history, culture, and knowledge that is often difficult to discover.

Marks, notations, and other marginalia <note>original: "marginalia"; refers to notes written in the margins of a book</note> present in the original volume will appear in this file—a reminder of this book’s long journey from the publisher to a library and finally to you.

## Usage guidelines

Google is proud to partner with libraries to digitize public domain materials and make them widely accessible. Public domain books belong to the public, and we are merely their custodians. Nevertheless, this work is expensive; therefore, in order to keep providing this resource, we have taken steps to prevent abuse by commercial parties, including placing technical restrictions on automated querying.

We also ask that you:

+ **Make non-commercial use of the files:** We designed Google Book Search for use by individuals, and we request that you use these files for personal, non-commercial purposes.

+ **Refrain from automated querying:** Do not send automated queries of any sort to Google’s system. If you are conducting research on machine translation, optical character recognition <note>OCR: the technology used to convert images of text into editable digital text</note>, or other areas where access to a large amount of text is helpful, please contact us. We encourage the use of public domain materials for these purposes and may be able to help.

+ **Maintain attribution:** The Google “watermark” you see on each file is essential for informing people about this project and helping them find additional materials through Google Book Search. Please do not remove it.

+ **Keep it legal:** Whatever your use, remember that you are responsible for ensuring that what you are doing is legal. Do not assume that just because we believe a book is in the public domain for users in the United States, that the work is also in the public domain for users in other countries. Whether a book is still under copyright varies from country to country, and we cannot offer guidance on whether any specific use of any specific book is allowed. Please do not assume that a book’s appearance in Google Book Search means it can be used in any manner anywhere in the world. Copyright infringement liability can be quite severe.

Google’s mission is to organize the world’s information and to make it universally accessible and useful. ### SKETCH OF LIFE.

Dionysius was born<sup>1</sup> in Athens in 5 A.D., nine years after the birth of Jesus Christ. He was educated first at Athens and then at Heliopolis in Egypt. There, at the age of twenty-five, he witnessed—along with Apollophanes—the miraculous darkness<sup>2</sup> of the sun which occurred at the time of the Crucifixion in 30 A.D.<sup>3</sup> Having returned to Athens, he married a woman of noble birth named Damaris and was elected one of the nine Archons <note>original: "Archons"; the chief magistrates or high-ranking officials of Athens</note> of the city. Having filled the office of Archon without reproach, he was elected a member of the Areopagus, a court of justice consisting of fifty-one citizens of Athens who were distinguished for their birth, wealth, intelligence, and character. 

In 49 A.D.,<sup>4</sup> nineteen years after the Crucifixion and in the ninth year of the reign of Emperor Claudius, St. Paul was brought before the court of the Areopagus as a "proclaimer of strange gods" because he was preaching about Jesus and the Resurrection. The Court of the Areopagus was the guardian of the religion of Athens; it had previously condemned Socrates to death on the grounds that, by teaching that one God was supreme, he dishonored the gods of Greece. The Athenians, however, were so ashamed of their treatment of that great philosopher that no person afterward was subjected to the same fate for the same cause. Hence, St. Paul escaped. 

Nor was this all. Dionysius, along with other illustrious men, stayed close to Paul and embraced the Christian Faith. The primary cause of his conversion was learning from St. Paul that the darkness he had observed in Egypt happened at the same time <note>original: "coeval"</note> as the Crucifixion. He was baptized on October 3rd, 49 A.D., in the forty-fourth year of his age. St. Paul left Athens soon after, accompanied by Dionysius, who was his companion for three years.<sup>6</sup> During this time, Dionysius was prepared for the Diaconate <note>the office of a deacon</note> and formed that intimate friendship with Timothy which lasted until death. Dionysius was present with Hierotheus, St. Paul, St. Peter, St. James, St. John, and other Apostles at the funeral rites of the Blessed Virgin, who died at Jerusalem in 54 A.D.,<sup>7</sup> twenty-four years after the Ascension, in the seventy-second year of her age. Dionysius was, in due time, ordained a Priest. We do not know the exact date, but we gather the fact from—

<sup>6</sup> page 281. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; <sup>7</sup> page 293.

<keywords>Dionysius the Areopagite, Athens, Heliopolis, St. Paul, Areopagus, conversion, solar darkness, early church history</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the life and works of Dionysius the Areopagite, focusing on his ecclesiastical roles and travels.</meta>

# INTRODUCTION.

In his letter to Demophilus, Dionysius suggests that he had admitted Demophilus to the order of the *Therapeutae* <note>original: "Therapeutæ"; a term for an early ascetic Christian community or "servants of God"</note>. It was the priest’s function alone to admit members to that order. Dionysius appears to have been a member of a College of Clergy in Athens, whose duty was to provide instruction to candidates for baptism, to study higher theology, and to share such information as was needed by the Church at large <margin>See the *Celestial Hierarchy*, chapter 13, near the end.</margin>. Dionysius of Corinth says that he took the oversight <note>original: "τὴν ἐπισκοπὴν" (ten episkopen); the office of a bishop or overseer</note> of the Parish of Athens <margin>Eusebius, *Ecclesiastical History*, chapter 23.</margin>. He also wrote letters to individuals who sought explanations of statements contained in his circulated works. 

He appears to have traveled to other centers. He was absent from Athens when Demophilus was guilty of the act of insubordination which prompted his instructive letter of rebuke <margin>See Letters.</margin>. That same letter alludes to his visit to Crete, where he was the guest of Carpus, who afterwards became Bishop of the Troad <note>See 2 Timothy 4:13</note>. Dionysius was ordained Bishop of Athens by St. Paul, succeeding Hierotheus, who most probably accompanied St. Paul on his first journey to Spain in A.D. 59—the sixth year of Nero’s reign. 

Before his martyrdom, St. Paul sent for Dionysius to come to Rome to take charge of the congregations Paul had planted in Gaul <note>Modern-day France</note>. Dionysius arrived too late to see the Apostle alive, but Clement <note>Pope Clement I</note> confirmed the commission. Dionysius, along with Rusticus, Eleutherius, and others, departed for Gaul. They arrived first at Arles, where Regulus was appointed Bishop to succeed Trophimus <margin>2 Timothy 4:20.</margin>, who had returned to Asia due to illness. 

Dionysius and his two companions ultimately established themselves in *Lutetia Parisiorum* <note>The Roman name for Paris</note>, which was then the center of trade between Britain and the Mediterranean. He remained there as a center of operations until A.D. 96, when he returned to Athens to meet St. John after his release from the island of Patmos. St. John and Dionysius met at Ephesus to "speak face to face," and it is probable that the last chapter was then added to St. John’s Gospel. The tenth letter of Dionysius clearly implies that St. John’s Gospel was already in circulation and had been read by Dionysius. The last chapter was added to correct a prevalent impression in the Church that St. John would not die. 

Dionysius remained in Asia for two years. Before his return to Gaul, St. Timothy died during the reign of Nerva (A.D. 97–98), as affirmed by Polycrates, a second-century writer and Bishop of Ephesus. It is probable that Dionysius revised and completed his works during that time. Upon his return to Gaul, Dionysius presented a copy of the *Divine Names* to Eugenius Marcellus, treating him as a "second Timothy" in pious memory of the first <margin>Volume 2, Antiquarian Edition, page 431.</margin>.

Through his sanctity, learning, and venerable age, Dionysius...

<keywords>Dionysius the Areopagite, St. Paul, St. John, Gaul, Athens, Lutetia Parisiorum, Church History, Apostolic Age</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction regarding the life and legacy of Dionysius the Areopagite, focusing on his martyrdom under the Emperor Hadrian and the historical authenticity of his writings.</meta>

# INTRODUCTION.

Dionysius acquired a commanding influence in Spain, Germany, and Britain (in all the Western regions <note>original Greek: πᾶσι τοῖς Ἑσπερίοις μέρεσι</note>). The Druid priests <margin>pages 335-9</margin> made desperate efforts to thwart him. His influence became known in Imperial Rome. Nearly the whole of the last years of the Emperor Trajan <margin>See Trajan and Hadrian in *Smith's Dictionary of Greek and Roman Biography*</margin> were spent in the East for the subjugation of the Parthian and other kingdoms. In A.D. 115, he left Syria for the Parthian expedition. In August, A.D. 117, he died at Selinus in Cilicia on his return to Rome. 

Hadrian was proclaimed Emperor at Antioch on August 11th, 117. He at once arranged matters in the East and arrived at Rome in 118 A.D., where, by concessions to the Senate and gifts to the people, he consolidated his position. In A.D. 119, he began his tour of the provinces by passing through Gaul on his way to Britain. He then sent the Prefect of Gaul to allure Dionysius from Christianity to Paganism. If the Prefect succeeded, he was to bring Dionysius with honor into the Imperial presence. If he failed, he was to cut off his head. 

Dionysius rejected the Imperial honors and chose the crown of martyrdom. With his two companions—united in life and not divided in death—he joyfully sealed his testimony in his own blood and "departed to Christ" <note>original Latin: *migravit ad Christum*</note> on October 9th, A.D. 119, <margin>page 341</margin> in the seventieth year of his Christian service and in the one hundred and fourteenth year of his natural life. The hill on which he suffered is therefore called Montmartre <note>literally "Mount of the Martyrs"</note>. 

In order to terrorize Dionysius and his companions, Lisbius—the first convert <note>original: "first-fruits"</note> of Gaul—along with his repentant wife, suffered martyrdom a few days previously. Because of this, the noble house of Montmorency <margin>*The History of the House of Montmorency* by M. Desarmeaux, Vol. I, p. 12. Paris, 1764. Antwerp Edition, 2nd Vol., 317.</margin> bears the title "First Christian Baron" <note>original French: *Premier Baron Chretien*</note> as his representatives to this day. It is, perhaps, more than a coincidence that Monseigneur Darboy, the Archbishop of Paris—who had written so nobly and bravely in defense of the writings of Dionysius and had translated them into French—suffered death as Archbishop in the same city, if not on the same mount, on May 24th, 1871.

The Eastern Church, naturally, commemorates the Baptism of Dionysius on the 3rd of October. The Western Church, just as naturally, commemorates his martyrdom on the 9th of the same month.

***

### THE WORKS OF DIONYSIUS.

If forged <note>original: "spurious"</note> works that threw light upon the doctrines and services of the Church in Apostolic times had been found in an Egyptian tomb, what a stir the discovery would have made! These works—

<language>English</language>
<page-num>4</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION.</header>
<page-type>preface</page-type>

[These writings] have been buried <note>original: "entombed"</note> in public libraries for the last 150 years—known and read by only a chosen few. They are not fakes <note>original: "spurious"</note>, though their authenticity may lessen their interest for some. Furthermore, they show that the New Testament was in circulation and considered Divinely authoritative as early as the first century. This fact will also diminish their value for those Christian defenders who try to present the Christian Faith under unbelievable conditions, and whose logical goal is to insist on both the necessity and the folly of Faith. Others may claim they are forgeries. However, we must remain faithful to the Truth and to the glorious "men of old," affirming that these are the genuine works of Dionysius the Areopagite—the convert and faithful disciple of St. Paul. 

The writings of Dionysius are a collection of letters, written at various intervals between A.D. 58 and 98. The larger treatises are addressed to Timothy. Dionysius was the head of a college of teachers <note>original: "Preceptors"</note>¹ in Athens, whose work was the instruction of new converts and the study of advanced theology. They were taught the deeper mysteries of the Christian Faith by Hierotheus, the first local Bishop² of Athens. Hierotheus wrote several abstract works, similar in style to Aristotle’s *Ethics*, which were too profound and condensed to be understood by ordinary minds.³ At the request of Timothy, and by order of Hierotheus, Dionysius wrote *The Divine Names* in order to present the teaching of Hierotheus in a more understandable form. 

The letters were begun while both Dionysius and Timothy were still elders <note>original: "Presbyters"</note>, and the original titles <note>original: "superscription"</note> were kept for the completed treatises. Is this not the case with nearly all the Prophets and with some of St. Paul’s Epistles? When Dionysius writes to St. John, Polycarp, or Titus, he addresses St. John as "Theologian" <note>original Greek: *Theologos*</note>—meaning Apostle or Evangelist—the others as "Hierarch" <note>original Greek: *Hierarches*</note>, and Gaius as "Attendant" <note>original Greek: *Therapeutes*</note>. The appeal to Timothy’s judgment as a Bishop <note>original: "Episcopal judgment"</note> in the ninth chapter of *The Celestial Hierarchy* shows that Timothy had become a Bishop by the time that letter was written. It is worth noting that this title is not attached to *The Mystic Theology*—the last of his works—even though it was written to his "friend Timothy." The complete works probably received their finishing touches between A.D. 96 and 98, during the two years that Dionysius spent in Asia when he met St. John at Ephesus.

4th: Publius, from A.D. 118–124; 
5th: Quadratus, who presented an *Apology* <note>A formal written defense of the Christian faith</note> to the Emperor Hadrian.
**3** *Divine Names*, chapter 3, section 2.

<page-num>5</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION.</header>

### EVIDENCE OF DATE IN WRITINGS.

The works are full of personal references. Dionysius affirms that he was converted to the Christian faith by St. Paul, whose teaching on the holy angels he passes on. After St. Paul, he claims Hierotheus as his chief instructor. He attempts to make the abstract treatises of Hierotheus more understandable while preserving his doctrine with the utmost fidelity. He also declares himself to have been present at the funeral rites of the Blessed Virgin Mary, who died in Jerusalem in A.D. 54. He mentions that Peter, James, and other Apostles were present at that event. He reminds Timothy that, among those following the Apostles, Hierotheus surpassed everyone else in the hymns he used on that occasion to praise the Divine compassion and humility shown in the Incarnation <note>The belief that God became human in Jesus Christ</note>. 

He writes to St. John while he was exiled on the island of Patmos; to Titus while he was Bishop of Crete; and to Polycarp while he was Bishop of Smyrna. He mentions Gaius and Carpus, both of whom are named in the New Testament. He mentions a philosopher named Clement—referring either to Clement Atrius (to whom the younger Pliny addressed two of his letters) or Clement, the Bishop of Rome. 

He writes during a period when the Psalms were still read in Hebrew whenever a person capable of doing so was present. He describes the chants as being sung by the entire choir. Antiphonal singing <note>A style of singing where two groups alternate parts</note> was introduced by St. Ignatius at Antioch and had become common practice by the time of Emperor Constantine. He also writes at a time when the threefold ministry (deacons, priests, and bishops) and the sacraments were the accepted order of the Church, before the "sevenfold" view of sacraments became dominant at the end of the second century. He speaks of persecutions, martyrdoms, and "Apostolic Sees" <note>original: "Sees"; administrative centers or headquarters established by the Apostles</note> that were already being led by the successors of the Apostles.

A man named Apollophanes once wrote a letter $^1$ insulting Dionysius as a "turncoat" who used Greek learning against Greek philosophy. Dionysius replies that Polycarp should remind Apollophanes that both of them had together witnessed a miraculous darkness in Egypt. Dionysius discovered that this darkness happened at the exact same time <note>original: "coeval"</note> as the Crucifixion of Christ. 

The works are written in an Athenian style <note>original: "Attic"</note> and are filled with technical terms from Athenian law. They also feature many newly created words that express Christian truths with great precision using the language of Plato and Aristotle. To use the perfect description provided by Bishop Westcott, "they $^2$ present the thoughts of one who lived in an age of transition, and strove to save from the wreck of ancient philosophy truths which he seemed to find consistent with the Christian Faith." While gladly acknowledging the truth found in Greek philosophy, he

---

The messengers carrying the letter from the Church at Corinth, who were complaining about false teachers, found St. Paul in chains at Philippi. See *Correspondence of St. Paul*, Carrière and S. Berger, page 20. Published by Fischbacher, Paris.
$^2$ *Religious Thought in the West*, p. 189.

---

corrects its errors by teaching that "the universe was created, not by any subordinate or rival power, but by an act of love of the One Infinite God, and that evil therefore is not inherent in matter, but due to the will of responsible creatures. He maintains that God is immanent <note>original: "imminent"</note> in the world, and separate, though not alien from it. That the world was originally and essentially good."

Furthermore, the "Ecclesiastical Hierarchy" <note>original: "Ecclesiastical Hierarchy," one of the author's primary works on church structure</note> was written during a time when the Bishop was the main Celebrant and completed every other Sacrament by the administration of the Holy Communion. However, the celebration was beginning to be entrusted to the Priest—a transition occurring when the Bishop officiated at funerals and decided, by his own authority, whether the deceased had lived and died in such a manner as to justify the use of Christian burial rites and benefit from them. 

This was also a time when the Bishop alone consecrated the holy oil, and when the names of those who had previously been buried were recited at a funeral,² a practice which soon became impossible to continue. Dionysius wrote at a time when the term *Bishop* <note>original: "Episcopus"</note>³ was not yet applied to the office in the modern sense. Instead, he uses the word *Hierarch* <note>original: "Hierarches"</note>, meaning head of the hierarchy, for Bishop; *Priest* <note>original: "Hiereus"</note> for Priest; and *Minister* <note>original: "Leitourgos," here referring to a Deacon</note> for Deacon. 

Dionysius regards the laborious search after truth through intellectual study as only an inferior method. It is much lower in degree than the method by which we detach ourselves from everything earthly and lie passive in the presence of God. In this state, without mental effort, we receive the divine communication of light as our spiritual condition permits, increasing as we advance more and more towards oneness with God. To him, prayer is better than reading, and resting under the surrounding glory of God is better than all else. Everything false or evil places a barrier to the passage of the Divine Light, which will not enter until the "strong man armed" <note>A biblical reference to Luke 11:21, referring here to spiritual obstacles or the ego</note> is cast out and one's allegiance to God becomes entire. Such is the teaching of Dionysius, who writes throughout with the freedom and fearlessness of one conscious of the truth.

---

### EXTERNAL EVIDENCE.

*First Century.*—St. Luke records the conversion at Athens of certain illustrious men and singles out one of them by name—Dionysius the Areopagite. He thus marks Dionysius as pre-eminently distinguished. Hierotheus was probably one of those illustrious men. He was an Areopagite, just like Dionysius. As Dionysius was sent to Gaul <note>Modern-day France</note>, so Hierotheus was sent to Spain. He settled permanently in Segovia in A.D. 69.⁴ If then, Hierotheus—

---

¹ *Ecclesiastical Hierarchy*, chapter 5, section 5; chapter 6, *Sacrament* <note>original: "Mysterion"</note>.
² *Ecclesiastical Hierarchy*, chapter 7, *Sacrament* <note>original: "Mysterion"</note>.
³ The term *Bishop* <note>original: "Episcopus"</note> applies to external government—*Hierarch* <note>original: "Hierarches"</note> applies to spiritual administration.
⁴ Pierre Halloix, *The Life of Hierotheus*.

<summary>This page analyzes the theological and structural evidence in Dionysius's writings, noting his focus on God's immanence and a mystical, passive approach to divine light. It also notes the archaic ecclesiastical titles he uses and begins the external historical evidence for his life and that of his contemporary, Hierotheus.</summary>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument for the early authorship of the Dionysian writings, attempting to prove they date to the first century by citing references and commentaries from early church figures.</meta>

<page-num>7</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION.</header>

If Dionysius was one of the "illustrious" men from whom he is distinguished by name, there must have been some reason for such a distinction. The treatise on the *Divine Names* was written about 58 AD. The ninth chapter of the *Celestial Hierarchy* was written when Timothy was already a Bishop. The Acts of the Apostles ends with St. Paul's imprisonment at Rome in 65 AD. Those treatises, therefore, must have been known to St. Paul and to St. Luke before 65 AD. From preserved letters asking the meaning of certain phrases in the writings of Dionysius, we know that his works were widely circulated. I submit, then, that the reason which led St. Luke to distinguish Dionysius the Areopagite <note>A member of the Areopagus, the supreme council of Athens; Dionysius was a judge there before his conversion by St. Paul.</note> by name was not merely his noble birth,¹ deep learning, and high position, but also the value of the works he was writing. These were worthy—like those of Hierotheus—to be considered second only to the Apostles because they contained Apostolic teaching. Literary criticism may fairly suggest a likely proof that Dionysius at least wrote *some* celebrated works. Plato was described by Numenius, a Pythagorean philosopher, as "Moses speaking Attic Greek." <note>Attic Greek was the prestigious dialect of ancient Athens; the quote suggests Plato’s philosophy was a Greek expression of divine truth found in the Hebrew scriptures.</note> If we find Dionysius writing Christian truths in Attic Greek, it is only what we might reasonably expect.

*Second Century.* — Ignatius² in his letter to the Trallians lists some of the deep mysteries of the Christian Faith regarding the holy angels, the span of the ages, the pre-eminence of the Cherubim and Seraphim, the lifting up of the Spirit, the Kingdom of the Lord, and the incomparable nature of the all-powerful God. He not only quotes the very words found in Dionysius but shows a thorough familiarity with his works. In the *Celestial Hierarchy* and in the *Divine Names*, the mysteries mentioned by Ignatius are exhaustively described and explained. What Ignatius merely hints at, Dionysius writes an entire treatise upon in the sixth chapter of the *Celestial Hierarchy* and the twelfth of *Divine Names*. We must therefore conclude that Ignatius quotes from Dionysius, whose works were therefore in circulation in the early part of the second century.

*Third Century.* — Anastasius of Antioch³ and Maximus⁴ the Martyr both affirm that Dionysius, the famous Bishop of Alexandria, wrote commentaries <note>original: "scholia," referring to explanatory notes or scholarly comments written in the margins of ancient manuscripts</note> upon the works of Dionysius the Areopagite. Maximus, in his commentary on the fifth chapter of the *Celestial Hierarchy*, when speaking upon the word *essences* <note>original: "οὐσίας" (ousias), referring to the fundamental nature or substance of being</note> as applied in that chapter to the holy Angels, says, "The great Dionysius, the Bishop of Alexandria, who had been a teacher of rhetoric, in the commentaries <note>original: "scholia"</note> which he made upon the blessed Dionysius, his..."

² Read *Ante-Nicene Edition*, Vol. 2, pages 390-396 and page 450.
³ Page 452.
⁴ Vol. 1, page 70.

<summary>This page argues that the writings of Dionysius the Areopagite date back to the first century, citing their supposed influence on St. Paul, St. Luke, Ignatius of Antioch, and Dionysius of Alexandria.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the chronological defense of the authenticity of the writings of Dionysius the Areopagite, citing witnesses from the third through the sixth centuries to show that his works were accepted by the early Church Fathers.</meta>

<page-num>8</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION.</header>

Maximus <note>Likely Maximus the Confessor (c. 580–662 AD), a prominent theologian and commentator on Dionysius.</note>, his namesake, speaks in this way: "Secular philosophy was accustomed to call every invisible nature 'uncreated,' and to call the individual persons 'beings' <note>original: "ἀγέννητον πᾶσαν ἀόρητον φύσιν, ὁμοίως καὶ οὐσίας τὰς ὑποστάσεις"—meaning "all invisible nature uncreated, and similarly, the persons as substances."</note>—and because of this, he says that expressions of this kind are used by the holy Dionysius metaphorically <note>original: "καταχρηστικῶς" (katachrestikos), meaning used in a non-technical or borrowed sense.</note>, after the manner of the secular philosophers"—the Greeks. 

Maximus is writing a commentary on Dionysius the Areopagite and refers to a specific word in the text. He says that Dionysius, the Bishop of Alexandria <note>Dionysius of Alexandria (died 264 AD), known as "the Great," was a prominent bishop and student of Origen.</note>, when commenting on the use of that word, affirms that the divine Dionysius the Areopagite uses the word in the same sense as the Greek philosophers, rather than in a strictly technical Christian sense. Maximus further proves that this Dionysius of Alexandria was the famous third-century bishop by describing him as having been a rhetorician—a well-known characteristic<sup>1</sup> of that bishop, who was a student of Origen.

*Fourth Century.*—The First Council of Nicaea<sup>2</sup> <note>The first ecumenical council of the Christian Church, held in 325 AD.</note> directs that when receiving a lapsed person <note>A "lapsed" person (lapsi) was a Christian who had renounced their faith or performed pagan sacrifices under the pressure of Roman persecution.</note> back into the Church, the prayer of Dionysius the Areopagite should be used. That prayer is mentioned in the last chapter of the *Ecclesiastical Hierarchy*. The Fathers of Nicaea, therefore, regarded Dionysius the Areopagite as an authority on church matters; they considered him so well-known that they did not think it was necessary to write out the prayer in full. In the same century, Dara, a learned Syrian theologian, wrote a commentary on the writings of Dionysius the Areopagite. The Syrian Church was one of the most intellectually advanced branches of the Church. Volumes of incredibly rare theological knowledge written in Syriac now lie hidden away in our libraries.

*Fifth Century.*—Photius<sup>3</sup>, the Patriarch of Constantinople, describes a book written by Theodore (around 400 AD), in which Theodore answers the arguments raised by some people against the authenticity of Dionysius's works. Photius believed those objections were not convincing. In discussing the official letter of Saint Sophronius, Photius says, "There were found collected the testimonies of Ancient Fathers: Leo... and Dionysius, the disciple of Saint Paul, a martyr for Christ, and the Bishop of Athens—great in his style of expression, but even greater in the depth of his thought."

*Sixth Century.*—In the sixth century, Gregory<sup>4</sup> the Great not only quotes Dionysius in a way that shows he was intimately familiar with his works, but he also describes him as: "Dionysius the Areopagite, the ancient and venerable Father." Gregory’s own writings so closely reflect the spirit of Dionysius that some critics—desperate to credit the works to anyone other than Dionysius himself—have foolishly suggested that Gregory the Great might have written them and then humbly attributed them to Dionysius. Gregory would at least have found that suggestion amusing.

<sup>1</sup> Smith's *Dictionary of Greek and Roman Biography and Mythology*.
<sup>2</sup> *Ancient Edition*, Vol. 2, p. 461.  &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; <sup>3</sup> p. 395. &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; <sup>4</sup> p. 462.

<meta>This page continues the chronological argument for the authenticity of the writings of Dionysius the Areopagite, moving from the seventh century through the Middle Ages and into the modern era, listing significant theologians who supported his work and those who questioned it.</meta>

<page-num>9</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION.</header>

***Seventh Century.***—The second Council of Nicaea <gloss>1</gloss> <note>The Seventh Ecumenical Council, held in 787 AD, which restored the use of icons.</note> quotes the very words of Dionysius, referring to him as "the great Dionysius" <note>original: ὁ μέγας Διονύσιος</note>, and says "that the divinely transmitted oracles are the very essence of our hierarchy, that is, in the true science and knowledge of things divine." "For the essence of our hierarchy consists of the divinely transmitted oracles" <note>original: οὐσία γὰρ τῆς καθ’ ἡμᾶς Ἱεραρχίας ἐστὶ τὰ θεοπαράδοτα Λόγια</note>. (*Ecclesiastical Hierarchy*, chapter 1, section 4).

***Eighth Century.***—The one man whose opinion upon the works of Dionysius is entitled to the greatest consideration and confidence is St. John Damascene <gloss>2</gloss> <note>A Syrian monk and priest (c. 675–749 AD), considered the last of the Greek Fathers.</note>. As the most learned of the Greek ecclesiastical writers, he left, among other numerous works, an *Exposition of the Orthodox Faith*, gathered from the writers of previous centuries, with whom he was deeply familiar. He regards Dionysius as the first and best of all. He frequently quotes his exact words and considers his authority as only second to that of the Apostles. St. Thomas Aquinas made known to the West the treasures of theological learning contained in St. John Damascene, and speaks of these works with the same confidence as those of Dionysius the Areopagite, the convert and faithful disciple of St. Paul. It must suffice to say that Maximus <note>Maximus the Confessor</note> and Pachymera <note>George Pachymeres, a 13th-century Byzantine historian and philosopher.</note> wrote *Scholia* <term>Scholia: ancient grammatical, critical, or explanatory comments on a text, written in the margins of manuscripts.</term>, which remain to this day as a testimony of their belief in the genuineness and value of Dionysius. Five Greek Fathers wrote biographies and accounts of the martyrdom of the Areopagite. 

Time and space would fail to tell of St. Ephrem and Constantine the Philosopher, John of Salisbury and Robert of Lincoln <note>Robert Grosseteste</note>, Peter Lombard and Alexander of Hales, William of Paris and Vincent of Beauvais, St. Anselm and Albert the Great, Bernard of Clairvaux and Scotus Erigena, Bonaventure and Gerson, Dionysius the Carthusian and Thomas à Kempis, Dean Colet and Jacques Lefèvre d’Étaples, Milton and Dante, Bessarion and Budé, Darboy and Pusey, Dom Pitra and Dulac—all of whom have either written commentaries upon, translated, or reproduced the doctrine of this Apostolic man.

But has there been no discordant note to mar the celestial harmony of this illustrious testimony? Oh, indeed there has! The Middle Ages <gloss>3</gloss> proclaimed their wisdom by their rejection of Origen. This modern time displays its "advance" in learning through its ignorance of Dionysius. Here are some of the names that have been rescued from obscurity by their crude objections to the genuineness of the writings of Dionysius: Grocyn <note>William Grocyn (c. 1446–1519), an English humanist who was one of the first to publicly challenge the Dionysian authorship.</note>, Scultetus, Scaliger, Baumgarten, Morinus, and Daillé. These are the distinguished names under whose cover modern scholars rely with such confidence that they complacently regard anyone as ignorant of the early centuries if their boldness allows them to affirm, in the nineteenth century, that the writings of Dionysius are genuine.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of scholars from the Renaissance and Reformation periods who questioned the authenticity of the writings of Dionysius the Areopagite, including the critiques of William Grocyn, Abraham Scultetus, Joseph Scaliger, and Siegmund Baumgarten.</meta>

<page-num>10</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION.</header>

---

Grocinus <note>original: Grocinus. Refers to William Grocyn (c. 1446–1519), an English scholar and friend of Erasmus who famously changed his mind about the authenticity of the Dionysian writings.</note> thought the works were not genuine, but with the instinct of a scholar, he carefully avoided giving any reason as proof by which the value of his arguments <note>original: "groceries." This may be a sarcastic term used by the author to refer to Grocyn’s "intellectual stock," or an archaic/idiosyncratic usage.</note> might be tested.

Scultetus <note>original: Scultetus. Refers to Abraham Scultetus (1566–1624), a German Reformed theologian.</note> thought that because certain rites and ceremonies in the administration of the Sacraments are described so that their spiritual meaning may be explained, the works must have been written later than the time of the Areopagite. Scultetus, in spite of his refined knowledge of the Gospel, overlooked the fact that during the forty days after the Resurrection, our Savior instructed the Apostles in things concerning the Kingdom of God; that St. Paul established the order of Divine Service in the Churches he visited; and that Paul even quotes from an existing Liturgy in one of his Epistles. <note>1 Corinthians 11:26.</note> Dionysius spends about six pages describing the rites and ceremonies of Holy Communion, Baptism, and Chrism <term>consecrated oil used in religious rites</term>. Scultetus thought such rites and ceremonies implied an inappropriate elevation of Sacraments that had been instituted by Christ Himself. St. Paul said to his converts, “By one Spirit were you all baptized into one body;” and “As often as you eat this bread and drink of this cup, you proclaim the Lord’s death until He comes.” Baptism and the Lord’s Supper would undoubtedly not have held such an exalted position in "the Gospel according to St. Scultetus."

*Scaliger.*—Scaliger <note>Joseph Justus Scaliger (1540–1609), a prominent French religious scholar.</note> was a very devoted Episcopalian—when it served his purpose. Unlike some churchmen, he deemed the dignity of the office of the Bishop <note>original: Episcopate</note> its most essential feature, and a theological virtue to be most jealously guarded. Scaliger, therefore, could not believe that Dionysius the Areopagite—an older man and his chief instructor—would address Timothy by the endearing name of "child" <note>original: παῖϛ (pais)</note> or "my son." 

It is true that St. Paul tells Timothy to “let no man despise his youth,” and to hold his own. St. Paul sends Timothy on missions, but entreats Titus. But to suppose that Dionysius, who armed Timothy against the Greek philosophers of Ephesus, would address a fellow priest <note>original: presbyter</note> and a future Bishop as “My son,” showed a familiarity and affection that Scaliger felt was unbecoming to the dignity of the Episcopal order. Daillé <note>Jean Daillé (1594–1670), a French Protestant theologian.</note> could be very hard upon the Acephaloi <term>a name given to various Christian sects who had no "head" or recognized leader</term>, and Scaliger could be very "Episcopalian," when it served their purposes against the truth.

*Baumgarten.*—The pupils of the Baumgarten Academy <note>The school of Siegmund Jakob Baumgarten (1706–1757), a German theologian who applied rationalist philosophy to theology.</note> were taught that Dionysius was a Pagan who ennobled the Christian religion by introducing the secret doctrines of the Eleusinian mysteries <term>ancient Greek initiation ceremonies for the cult of Demeter and Persephone</term>. However, Dionysius speaks of himself <gloss>4</gloss> as being brought from darkness to light by his conversion to the Christian religion. In the logic of the Baumgarten Academy, the conclusion would appear to be something not necessarily supported by the facts.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of scholars who questioned the authenticity of the writings of Dionysius the Areopagite, moving from earlier critics to Jean Morin and Jean Daillé, and defending Dionysius against their arguments.</meta>

<page-num>11</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION.</header>

**Morinus.**—Morinus <note>original: Jean Morin (1591–1659), a French theologian and biblical scholar.</note> first proves that the works were written in Asia. Then, by ridiculous assumptions, he pretends to prove that they were written in Gaul. Then, as if initiating a new style of criticism and as proof of his own critical sharp-wittedness, he triumphantly asks: why, then, were they not written in Latin instead of Greek? He reveals the gaps in his own youthful education by failing to know that all early Christian literature was written in Greek, that Arles <note>A city in southern France.</note> was the center of Greek trade before it became a Roman colony, and that the Druids used Greek as their sacred language for writing <term>original: hierographic language</term>.

**Daillé.**—“Oh, that mine enemy would write a book,” <note>Job 31:35.</note> was the despairing hope of Job in the midst of his affliction. Dionysius, however, in spite of his martyrdom, was more fortunate in this respect. Daillé <note>original: Jean Daillé (1594–1670), a French Protestant minister known for his critical work on the writings of the Early Church Fathers.</note> earned for himself the reputation of those “who rush in where angels fear to tread.” He has the proud distinction of being the only man in the Christian world who wrote a book to prove that the writings of Dionysius and Ignatius were unknown to early Christians and were, therefore, spurious. He produces sixty-six so-called arguments (unhappily not 666), “and that as a final one” <note>original: id que ultimum</note>, as he says, to show that the Ignatian Epistles were unknown to any early Father or Historian of the Church. Given the assumption that every piece of evidence to the contrary is either a forgery or a fraud, it is easily done. 

Bishop Lightfoot <note>Joseph Barber Lightfoot (1828–1889), a renowned English theologian and Bishop of Durham who successfully defended the authenticity of the letters of Ignatius of Antioch.</note>, however, after a *more mature* examination, demonstrated the futility of Daillé's method regarding the Ignatian Epistles. He proved them to be the genuine writings of the Bishop and Martyr, despite the sixty-six statements to the contrary, whose very number—like an army in confusion—becomes a source of weakness. Daillé's method regarding Dionysius is the same, or even worse, than his treatment of Ignatius. He modestly produces only 42 arguments against Dionysius. 

Here is an example: To establish his assumed position that the writings of Dionysius were unknown before 532 AD, he must deny the assertion of the learned Anastasius and the martyr Maximus that Dionysius, the famous Bishop of Alexandria, wrote scholarly notes <gloss>original: scholia</gloss> upon them around 250 AD. Maximus, for the purpose of affirming with greater accuracy that the Dionysius who wrote the notes was indeed the famous Bishop of Alexandria, describes him as having been a rhetorician <note>An expert in formal speaking and writing.</note>. That description corresponds exactly with the account given of him in *Smith’s Dictionary of Greek and Roman Biography*.

St. Augustine had been much the same, as were also Justin Martyr and Ambrose of Milan. Daillé was a bitter Presbyterian, but he affects much consideration for the dignity of the—

<page-num>12</page-num>

# INTRODUCTION.

The office of a bishop <term>Episcopal office: the position or rank of a bishop</term> is such that Daillé cannot bring himself to believe that someone holding the exalted position of Bishop of Alexandria could have been described as a rhetorician <term>rhetorician: a teacher or practitioner of the art of persuasive speaking and writing</term>. Maximus <note>original: Maximus the Confessor (c. 580–662 AD), a prominent Christian monk and theologian.</note> merely says that he had been a rhetorician before his consecration. Daillé, having denied that Dionysius, Bishop of Alexandria, wrote the commentaries <note>original: scholia</note>, felt compelled to find some rhetorician named Dionysius to whom the commentaries on the Areopagite might be attributed. But such a man, he confesses, cannot be found—except, as he claimed, in the imagination of Maximus and Pachymera <note>original: George Pachymeres (1242–1310), a Byzantine historian and philosopher.</note>. He should have said: except in the imagination of Daillé. Having himself fabricated this imaginary rhetorician, he accuses Maximus the Martyr and the learned Pachymera of fraud by claiming they attributed the commentaries of this unknown man to the exalted Bishop of Alexandria in order to glorify their Dionysius the Areopagite. There is a famous saying by Tennyson: “We always impute ourselves” <note>Meaning we project our own faults or motives onto others.</note>. Another example of the forty-two—“and this the final one” <note>original: "id que ultimum"</note>—statements by Daillé against Dionysius must suffice: “From these two examples, judge them all” <note>original: "Ex duobus disce omnes"</note>. 

A Conference^1 was held at Constantinople in A.D. 532 between Asiatic and African theologians <note>original: Divines</note> to settle the controversies then disturbing the Church regarding the "hypostatic union" <term>hypostatic union: the theological doctrine of the union of Christ's divine and human natures in one individual person</term>, or the single personality of Christ. The Athanasian Creed describes that personality as “one altogether,” “not two.” It is a union in person, and not merely an alliance of two personalities. The Council of Chalcedon declares that out of two whole and perfect natures, there is one Christ. This was a deep mystery. Each side testified to a different aspect of the truth, and each side was liable to lean toward error—either Nestorianism <term>Nestorianism: the heresy that Christ existed as two separate persons, one human and one divine</term> or Monophysitism <term>Monophysitism (Monophytism): the heresy that Christ had only one nature, which was divine, rather than two</term>. 

The African clergy naturally quoted the phrase “divine-human activity” <note>original: θεανδρικὴν ἐνέργειαν (theandrikēn energeian)</note> from Dionysius as proving the one Christ. Hypatius <note>The Bishop of Ephesus, who led the debate for the "Orthodox" or "Catholic" side.</note>, who perhaps leaned toward that Nestorian party which had corrupted^2 the text of the Council of Chalcedon, was unable to resist the force of this expression. He suggested instead that the works might have been corrupted <note>Meaning altered or forged by others.</note>. They even grew so bold as to claim that the works were unknown to Cyril <note>Cyril of Alexandria (d. 444 AD), a major figure in these theological debates.</note> and were therefore probably fake. They were then informed that Cyril, in his book against Theodorus, had spoken highly of the works. They asked to see the book. They were told it was in the library at Alexandria, but that the African clergy had not thought it necessary to bring such a well-known book with them. There were at most five people in Hypatius's group, which Daillé calls “the Catholic party.” From these weak premises, Daillé draws the triumphant conclusion that the works of Dionysius were rejected by the entire *Catholic body*.

Fortunately for the truth, but unfortunately for Daillé, a council of both Greek and Latin bishops was held at the Lateran in A.D. 660^3 <note>This likely refers to the Lateran Council of 649, which dealt with these same theological issues.</note> to defeat the—

<meta>This page concludes the author's defense of the authenticity of the writings of Dionysius the Areopagite. It refutes specific historical criticisms from scholars like Valla and Erasmus and argues that the spiritual depth of the texts serves as the best evidence for their first-century origin.</meta>

<page-num>13</page-num>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<header>INTRODUCTION.</header>

...attempt of Cyrus to change **"new"** <note>original: *καινή* (*kainē*)</note> into **"one"** <note>original: *μίαν* (*mian*)</note> in the letter of Dionysius to Gaius. The works were produced from the Vatican Library, and the original text was examined, and the true reading was pronounced to be **"new"**. There was not a word said against the genuineness of the works, which were assumed to be beyond question the writings of Dionysius the Areopagite. So trustworthy is Daillé <note>Jean Daillé (1594–1670), a French Protestant theologian who argued the works were forgeries.</note> in saying that the writings attributed to Dionysius were repudiated by the whole **Catholic body**.

Laurentius Valla<sup>1</sup> says that many learned men in his day thought the works might have been written by Apollinaris the heretic <note>Apollinaris of Laodicea (d. 390 AD), whose Christological views were condemned as heretical.</note>. It would be impossible to find a more precise refutation of the heresy of Apollinaris than in the writings of Dionysius. Erasmus <note>Desiderius Erasmus (1466–1536), a famous Renaissance humanist who doubted the works' authenticity.</note> repudiated the opinion of Valla and said that he did not believe the works were written by Apollinaris or any other heretic. Erasmus said that some learned men thought the works were written by another Dionysius; but when challenged to produce the names of such learned men, he could only instance Valla and Grocinus. He was also careful not to say who the "other" Dionysius was. Erasmus apologized for the evident fraud—if the works were not written by Dionysius the Areopagite—by saying that "pious people were accustomed to commit forgery for the glory of God." It was a lamentable characteristic of that age, as of this one, that such idiotic blasphemy could be uttered without ruining the most illustrious reputation. Dupin<sup>2</sup> and Tillemont<sup>3</sup> seem to have followed such reasoning as I have indicated without serious examination. Such is the blind following of error in this century that all reasonable men are said to regard the writings of Dionysius as spurious. I cannot deny the fact, but I appeal to all rational men to decide whether that fact reflects credit upon the good sense and learning of the modern West.

For myself, grateful to Almighty God for the clear vision of the truth conveyed to my mind by these priceless works, I thankfully present them to the English people in my native tongue. They present the Christian Faith and the Christian Service as they existed in the first century, and before the Church was torn by schism <term>schism: a formal split or division within a religious body</term>, and its faith soiled by controversy. They show how an ideal Christianity may be realized in fact. They present the truth which men blindly grope after under the names of evolution, kenosis <term>kenosis: the "self-emptying" of Christ's own will to become entirely receptive to God's divine will</term>, theosophy <term>theosophy: any system of philosophy or mysticism that proposes to attain knowledge of God through spiritual ecstasy or direct intuition</term>, and ritualism. They show the true Atonement as that by which we become at one in ourselves by becoming one with God. They show the canonical Scriptures in circulation in the first century, and therefore as written by those whose names they bear. As with the Bible, so with Dionysius: the best evidence of genuineness is in the works themselves, devoutly read and...

---

<sup>3</sup> Volume 2, p. 236.

<page-num>14</page-num>

# INTRODUCTION.

intelligently understood. No church writer <note>original: "ecclesiastical writer"</note> knows his own mind so well, or expresses that mind so clearly, as Dionysius. No man states an objection so unflinchingly, or accepts the whole Bible as thoroughly as he. No man is so ready to admit that there are some things to be honored by silence, as being beyond the mind of man to conceive. No man realized, or expressed so clearly, the mutual coherence of the Old and New Testament as he. "The one [Old Testament] announced the divine works of Jesus that were to come, and the other [New Testament] fulfilled them" <note>original: *ἡ μὲν ἔφη τὰς ἐσομένας Ἰησοῦ θεουργίας, ἡ δὲ ἐτέλεσε* (hē men ephē tas esomenas Iēsou theourgias, hē de etelese)</note>—this phrase contains a volume of theology. No man shows such unfailing charity toward those who oppose the truth, or toward himself. No one so clearly presents the Church of Christ, the City of God, as a reflection of the divine order in God. Everything is a Trinity with him. Order is God’s first law. Goodness is the cause and origin of all things, and the mainstay of their continued existence. Evil is the failure to attain some imagined good—a falling from our original state <note>original: "first estate," a reference to the condition of angels or humans before the Fall</note>. Without some inherent good in itself, nothing could continue to exist.

->THANKS BE TO GOD.<-

<meta>This page begins the "Celestial Hierarchy" by Dionysius the Presbyter, following an introduction that established the author's theological clarity and his belief that the Church and the world reflect a divine, triune order.</meta>

<page-num>1</page-num>

# THE CELESTIAL HIERARCHY.

## CHAPTER 1.

->TO MY FELLOW ELDER <term>original: "presbyter," a leader or minister in the early church</term> TIMOTHY.<-
->DIONYSIUS THE ELDER.<-

*That every divine illumination, while going forth lovingly to the objects of its care <note>original: "forethought"</note> under various forms, remains simple <note>original: "simplex," meaning undivided or unified</note>. Nor is this all: it also unifies the things it illuminates.*

“EVERY good gift <gloss>1</gloss> and every perfect gift is from above, and comes down from the Father of Lights.”
Furthermore, every outpouring of illuminating light proceeding from the Father, while visiting us as a gift of goodness, restores us again through its unifying power to a higher spiritual state. It turns us toward the oneness of our guiding Father and to a deifying simplicity. For <gloss>2</gloss> all things are from Him and return to Him, as the Sacred Word <note>The Holy Scriptures</note> says. Invoking then Jesus—the Father’s Light, the Real, the True, “which lights <gloss>3</gloss> every man that comes into the world,” and “through <gloss>4</gloss> Whom we have access to the Father,” the Source of Light—let us aspire, as far as is allowed, to the illuminations handed down by our fathers in the most sacred scriptures. Let us gaze as best we can upon the Hierarchies of the heavenly minds <note>The different ranks of angels</note> manifested in those scriptures symbolically for our instruction. 

When we have received the gift of Light—primal and beyond all sources <note>original: "superprimal"</note>—from the supremely divine Father with an unearthly and steady mental vision, which shows us the blessed Hierarchies of the Angels in types and symbols, let us then be restored by this gift of Light back to its unique splendor.<gloss>5</gloss> For this light *never* loses its own inherent unity. Instead, as it spreads and goes forth to educate and unite the objects of its care (as fits its goodness), it remains firmly and uniquely centered within itself in its unchanging state. It elevates those who aspire to it according to their capacity, making them one after the example of its own unifying Oneness. For it is not possible that the Divine Ray should illuminate us in any other way, except insofar as it is wrapped in diverse veils for the purpose of sacred instruction, arranged naturally and appropriately for people like us by the Father's care.

---

1 James 1:17. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; 2 Romans 11:36. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; 3 John 1:9. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; 4 Romans 5:2.
5 Plato’s *Republic*, books 6 and 7 (pages 121-126 in some editions). See the "Allegory of the Cave."

<summary>Dionysius opens his treatise by explaining that all divine light comes from God and serves to unify and elevate humanity. He argues that because humans cannot perceive pure divine light directly, God uses "veils" of symbols and the angelic hierarchy to teach and guide us toward Him.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Celestial Hierarchy," specifically explaining why physical symbols and rituals are necessary for human beings to understand divine truths.</meta>

<page-num>16</page-num>

->CELESTIAL HIERARCHY.<-

Therefore, the sacred institution that established the Holy Rites passed down to us our most Holy Hierarchy. Having deemed it worthy to be a heavenly imitation of the angelic ranks, it depicted those non-physical <term>original: "immaterial"</term> Hierarchies using physical figures and bodily forms. This was done so that we might be carried—as much as our human capacity permits—from these sacred pictures to a mystical understanding and a likeness that requires no symbol or pattern. 

Our minds cannot be raised to that non-physical representation and contemplation of the Heavenly Hierarchies without using the physical guidance suited to our nature. We must regard visible beauty <gloss>1</gloss> as a reflection of invisible grace; the fragrant <gloss>2</gloss> scents of the senses as emblems of spiritual gifts; and physical <gloss>3</gloss> light as a likeness of the gift of non-physical enlightenment. We must see the clear, sacred instructions <gloss>4</gloss> as the mental feast of contemplation; and the ranks <gloss>5</gloss> of the orders here on earth as a reflection of the harmonious and disciplined character required for Divine things. Finally, we see the receiving of the most Divine Eucharist as the partaking <gloss>6</gloss> of Jesus. All these things are given to heavenly beings supernaturally, but to us, they are given through symbols.

For the sake of our deification <note>original: "deification," or *theosis*, the process of becoming more like God</note>—to the extent we are capable—the original institution of sacred mysteries, out of its love for humanity, revealed the Heavenly Hierarchies to us. It established our own Hierarchy to serve alongside them by imitating their God-like priesthood <gloss>7</gloss>. As far as we are able, the inspired scriptures <term>original: "oracles"</term> described these celestial minds using physical figures. This was done to lead us through the physical world to the spiritual, and from sacred symbols to the pure and lofty truths of the Heavenly Hierarchies.

***

### CHAPTER II. <note>original Latin: "CAPUT II"</note>

#### *That Divine and Heavenly things are appropriately represented, even through symbols that seem unlike them.*

I believe it is necessary, first, to explain the goal of every Hierarchy and the benefit each one provides to its followers. Next, we must honor the Heavenly Hierarchies as they are described in the scriptures. Then, following those scriptures, we should explain which sacred forms the divine descriptions use to depict the celestial orders, and what kind of plain truth we must find through these representations; in order that we—

<meta>This page continues the discussion of why the "Celestial Hierarchy"—the ranks of angels—are described in the Bible using physical symbols. The author argues that we should not take these descriptions literally, but understand them as tools for our human intelligence.</meta>

<page-num>17</page-num>

->CELESTIAL HIERARCHY.<-

...we should not, like the uneducated <note>original: "vulgar"</note>, irreverently think that the heavenly and God-like minds are a kind of four-footed <sup>1</sup> and many-faced <sup>2</sup> creatures. We must not think they are molded into the brutish form of oxen <sup>3</sup> or the savage form of lions, <sup>4</sup> or fashioned with the hooked beaks of eagles <sup>5</sup> or the feathery down of birds. <sup>6</sup> Nor should we imagine that there are certain kinds of fiery wheels <sup>7</sup> above the heavens, or material thrones <sup>8</sup> upon which the Divine Being <note>original: "Godhead"</note> may recline, or many-colored <sup>9</sup> horses and leaders of the army bearing spears, <sup>10</sup> or whatever else the scriptures <note>original: "oracles"</note> declare to us through various symbols for our instruction.

Indeed, the Word of God makes use of many poetic representations of sacred things in a straightforward way. It does this out of regard for our intelligence, so to speak, providing a method of education that is natural to our human way of thinking and shaping its instructive descriptions to fit us.

However, some might accept the sacred writings because they recognize that pure, spiritual <term>original: "uncompounded"</term> things are unknowable in their own nature and cannot be seen. Yet, these same people might think the imagery used for holy minds in the scriptures is unfitting <note>original: "incongruous"</note>. They might view all of this as a crude, theatrical representation of angelic names.

Such a person might argue that when theologians describe beings that have no bodies at all, they should represent them using forms that are as similar as possible to those honored, non-physical <term>original: "immaterial"</term>, and exalted beings. They might say that we should not clothe heavenly and God-like spirits <note>original: "essences"</note> with the forms of the lowest creatures found on earth. This other approach, they would argue, might be better for our education and would not degrade the explanation of heavenly things to such unfitting comparisons. They might fear that the current method does violence to the Divine powers and leads our minds astray by making us focus on these irreverent descriptions.

Furthermore, a person might think that the heavenly places are filled with herds of lions, troops of horses, bellowing songs of praise, flocks of birds, and other living creatures or less honorable things. They might feel that the comparisons in the scriptures, which are in every respect different from the things they describe, lean toward the absurd, the harmful, and the emotional. 

Now, in my opinion, investigating the truth demonstrates the sacred wisdom of the scriptures in these descriptions of the heavenly minds. That wisdom looks out for us, ensuring that it neither does violence to the Divine powers, nor leaves us trapped in a low, earthly...

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page explains why God and angels are described using strange, "dissimilar" images and "negations" (saying what God is NOT). The author argues that this protects sacred mysteries from the unworthy and more accurately reflects God's transcendent nature.</meta>

<page-num>18</page-num>

->**CELESTIAL HIERARCHY.**<-

...passions of the debased imagery. For someone might say that the reason why forms are naturally attributed to the formless, and shapes to the shapeless, is not just because of our own capacity—which is unable to rise immediately to spiritual <term>original: "intellectual"</term> contemplations and needs familiar instructions that present images of formless and supernatural things. Rather, it is also because it is most fitting for the revealing scriptures <note>original: "oracles"</note> to use unutterable and sacred riddles to conceal the holy and secret truth regarding the celestial minds, keeping it inaccessible to the common crowd. For not everyone is holy, nor, as the scriptures affirm, does knowledge belong to everyone.<sup>1</sup>

But if anyone criticizes these descriptions as being unfitting <note>original: "incongruous"</note>, and says it is shameful to attribute shapes so repulsive to the God-like and most holy Orders, it is enough to reply that the method of Divine revelation is twofold. One way, which is natural, represents things through "similar" likenesses of a sacred character. The other way uses "anomalous" <note>strange or irregular</note> shapes, fashioning them into total unlikeness and mismatch. 

No doubt, the mystical teaching of the revealing scriptures sometimes describes the majestic blessedness of the Godhead—who is beyond all essence <term>original: "super-substantial Deity"</term>—as **Word**,<sup>2</sup> **Mind**,<sup>3</sup> and **Being**,<sup>4</sup> showing its divine reason and wisdom, and demonstrating that it is truly existence and the true cause of the existence of all things. They also describe the Divine as **Light**.<sup>5</sup> While such sacred descriptions are more reverent and seem in some way superior to material images, even these, in reality, fall short of the supreme likeness of God. For the Divine is above all being and life. No light can truly express its character, and every description or mental concept falls incomparably short of its likeness.

But at other times, the scriptures themselves sing the praises of the Divine through *dissimilar* descriptions, when they affirm that God is **invisible**,<sup>6</sup> **infinite**,<sup>7</sup> and **incomprehensible**<sup>8</sup>—when what is made known is not what God *is*, but what God *is not*. I believe this is more appropriate, because, as the secret and Hierarchical tradition teaches, we are correct to describe God by his lack of relationship to created things. However, we do not know God's own indefinable nature, which is beyond all essence, thought, or words. 

If, then, "negations" <note>statements about what God is not</note> regarding Divine things are true, while "affirmations" <note>statements about what God is</note> are inadequate, then the method of explaining invisible things through dissimilar descriptions is more appropriate to the hidden mystery of unutterable things. In this way, the sacred descriptions of the scriptures honor, rather than shame, the celestial Orders when they make them known through dissimilar pictorial forms, demonstrating through these their supernatural...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on why the scriptures use "dissimilar" or "unlike" imagery—such as animals or base objects—to describe heavenly beings. The author argues that using beautiful images (like gold or light) might trick us into thinking angels are physical, whereas strange images force us to look for a deeper, spiritual meaning.</meta>

<page-num>19</page-num>

->**CELESTIAL HIERARCHY.**<-

...superiority to all material things. However, I do not believe any sensible person will deny that dissimilar representations raise our minds more effectively than "similar" ones. For with the more exalted representations of heavenly things, there is a risk that we might be misled into thinking that celestial Beings are simply certain types of creatures made of gold, or men appearing as light,¹ sparkling like lightning,² beautiful³ and dressed in bright, shining garments, radiating a harmless flame. This risk applies to all the other shapes and appropriate forms by which the Word of God has depicted these celestial minds.

To prevent people from mistakenly believing that these beings are nothing more than their beautiful appearance, the uplifting wisdom of the holy theologians <note>original: "pious theologians"</note> reverently leads us toward these "misleading" dissimilarities. This approach does not allow our lower, earthly nature to remain fixed on lowly images, but instead encourages the soul to reach upward, goading it by the very strangeness <note>original: "unseemliness"</note> of the symbols. For even to the most basic human understanding, it is neither right nor plausible that the most heavenly and Divine subjects of our contemplation are actually like these lowly things. 

Above all, this must particularly be borne in mind: nothing in existence is completely devoid of beauty. As the truth of the scriptures <note>original: "oracles"</note> clearly affirms, "all things are very beautiful."⁴ It is possible, therefore, to find good spiritual insights <note>original: "contemplations"</note> in everything, and to use material things to represent these "dissimilar likenesses" for both the spiritual truths and the spiritual beings <note>Dionysius uses "intelligible" for divine truths and "intelligent" for the minds or angels that perceive them.</note>. This is because spiritual beings experience things differently than do creatures of the senses. 

For instance, anger in irrational animals arises from their passions, and their angry behavior is completely unreasonable. But when we speak of spiritual beings, we must view "anger" differently. In my judgment, it signifies their vigorous <note>original: "masculine"</note> reason and their determined persistence in their God-like and unchanging steadfastness. Similarly, in irrational creatures, "lust" <note>original: "lust"</note> is a kind of reckless and earthly passion. It arises uncontrollably from an internal drive or an attraction to changing things, and it is the dominance of physical impulse that drives the whole body toward the object of sensual desire. 

However, when we attribute "lust" to spiritual beings by using these "dissimilar likenesses," we must understand it as a craving for immaterial things that are beyond words and thought. It is an unyielding and determined longing for heavenly, pure, and passionless contemplation, and for a perpetual, spiritual fellowship within that pure and most...

---

**¹** *Acts 1:10.* &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; **²** *Matthew 28:3.* &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; **³** *Acts 6:15.* &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; **⁴** *Genesis 1:31.*

<meta>This page continues the argument that "dissimilar" or "unlikely" symbols are actually more effective for describing spiritual realities. It transitions from discussing how to represent angels to how theologians use startling, lowly, and even animalistic imagery to describe the Godhead itself.</meta>

<page-num>20</page-num>

->**CELESTIAL HIERARCHY.**<-

exalted glory, and the enduring and beautifying loveliness. And what might be called "incontinence" <note>original: "incontinence"; here meaning an unrestrained or boundless desire</note> we can understand as a persistent and unyielding longing that nothing can turn away, driven by a pure and unchanging love for Divine beauty and a total orientation toward what is truly desirable. Regarding irrational animals or inanimate <note>original: "soulless"</note> matter, we correctly refer to their lack of reason and lack of feeling as a simple absence of those qualities. However, regarding immaterial and spiritual beings, we rightly acknowledge their superiority as heavenly beings. They exist far beyond our fleeting, physical language and our earthly perceptions, which are foreign to such bodiless minds. 

It is, therefore, acceptable to represent celestial beings using forms that do not seem appropriate to them, even using the lowliest parts of matter. After all, matter itself originated from the Essential Beauty <note>the source of all beauty, God</note>. Throughout all of material creation, there are echoes of spiritual loveliness. Through these material things, we can be led to immaterial archetypes <note>the original spiritual patterns</note>. As we have said, these comparisons are applied in a different way; the same symbols are defined not in a literal sense, but in a way that is harmonious and appropriate to the distinct natures of both spiritual and physical beings. Under these kinds of descriptions, we find that mystic theologians <note>ancient writers on the mysteries of God</note> use sacred symbolic sketches to depict not only the heavenly Orders, but sometimes even the Godhead <note>the essence of God</note> itself.

At times, they praise the Godhead using exalted imagery, such as the Sun <sup>1</sup> of Righteousness, the Morning <sup>2</sup> Star rising divinely within the mind, or the Light <sup>3</sup> that radiates a spiritual splendor that cannot be hidden. At other times, they use ordinary phenomena, like Fire <sup>4</sup> that gives off a harmless light, or Water <sup>5</sup> that provides the fullness of life—symbolically flowing into the body and bubbling up as rivers with irresistible power. In other instances, they use imagery of the lowliest type, such as sweet-smelling Myrrh <sup>6</sup> or a Chief Cornerstone <sup>7</sup>. They even describe the Godhead using the forms of wild beasts, giving it the qualities of a Lion <sup>8</sup> and a Panther <sup>9</sup>, or saying that it will be like a Leopard <sup>10</sup> and a rushing Bear <sup>11</sup>. I will even add what seems to be the lowliest and most misleading image of all: highly regarded theologians have shown us that the Divine even represents itself in the form of a worm <sup>12</sup>. 

In this way, the godly and wise—those who interpret secret inspiration—separate the most holy things from the uninitiated and the unholy to keep them pure. They prefer these "dissimilar" descriptions so that Divine truths are not easily grasped by the profane <note>those who treat sacred things with contempt or lack of understanding</note>, and so that those who diligently study the Divine imagery should

<meta>This page concludes the argument that "dissimilar" or "lowly" imagery is used by theologians to prevent readers from mistaking physical symbols for literal truth. It then transitions into a formal definition of "Hierarchy" as a sacred order intended to lead the soul toward union with God.</meta>

<page-num>21</page-num>

->CELESTIAL HIERARCHY.<-

we must not rest in these symbols as if they were literal truth. Thus, Divine things are honored through "negations" <note>original: "negations"; the theological practice of describing what God is NOT to emphasize His transcendence</note> which teach the truth, and through comparisons with the lowliest objects that are entirely different from their actual nature. For the reasons given, it is not absurd for the scriptures to depict even celestial beings using dissimilar likenesses that do not literally represent them. 

Perhaps we would never have sought a deeper investigation into Divine things—both because of the difficulty and for the sake of instruction—unless the strange and "deformed" descriptions of the Angels had shocked us. These descriptions do not allow us to linger on conflicting images but instead rouse us to reject earthly inclinations, training us to rise from visible things to their heavenly meanings. This is enough to say regarding the material and mismatched descriptions of the holy Angels in the Holy Scriptures. 

Next, we must define what we believe Hierarchy is in itself, and what benefits those within a Hierarchy receive from it. But let Christ lead this discussion, if I may say so—He who is my inspiration and the source of all Hierarchical teaching. But you, my son, following the pious rule of our Hierarchical tradition, listen with reverence to these holy words. Become inspired by these divine teachings. Once you have stored these Divine truths in the secret depths of your mind, guard them closely from the common crowd <note>original: "profane multitude"</note>. Keep them whole and unified, for it is not permitted—as the scriptures <note>original: "oracles"</note> say—to cast the pure, bright, and beautifying beauty of these spiritual pearls before swine <note>A reference to Matthew 7:6</note>.

---

### CHAPTER III. <note>original: "CAPUT III"</note>

### *What is Hierarchy? and what the use of Hierarchy?*

Hierarchy is, in my judgment, a sacred order, a science, and an activity <note>original: "energy"</note>. It is made like God as much as possible and is guided toward the illuminations granted to it by God. It follows a set order so that it may imitate the Divine. Now, the Divine beauty—because it is simple <note>original: "uncompounded"; meaning not made of parts</note>, good, and the source of all initiation—is completely free from any variation or difference. However, it shares its own light with each being according to their capacity, perfecting them through a most Divine initiation, based on how closely those being initiated reflect God's own likeness and harmony. 

The goal of Hierarchy, then, is to become like God and achieve union with Him. It holds Him as the Leader of all religious science and activity, looking steadily toward His most Divine beauty. It seeks to mold itself as far as possible into His image and to perfect its own...

<summary>The author explains that shocking or "lowly" descriptions of angels are intended to force the mind toward spiritual truths rather than literal interpretations. He then begins a formal definition of "Hierarchy" as a sacred system designed to lead individuals toward imitation of and union with God.</summary>

<keywords>Hierarchy, Angels, Divine Imitation, spiritual pearls, initiation, sacred order, symbolism, Pseudo-Dionysius</keywords>

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues the definition of "Hierarchy," describing it as a sacred order designed to reflect Divine Beauty and lead its members toward becoming "fellow-workers with God" through a structured process of spiritual growth.</meta>

<page-num>22</page-num>

->CELESTIAL HIERARCHY.<-

followers who act as Divine images—mirrors that are bright and flawless, ready to receive the original light and the Divine ray. They are devoutly filled with the radiance entrusted to them, but they also generously spread this light to those who follow them, according to the regulations established by God. For neither those who initiate others into Divine matters nor those being religiously initiated are allowed to act outside the sacred rules of their specific roles. Nor should they even attempt to do so if they desire to reach its deifying splendor, if they view it with religious reverence, and if they model themselves after the example of the holy minds. Therefore, anyone who speaks of "Hierarchy" describes a completely Holy Order—an image of Divine Beauty. This order performs the mysteries of its own enlightenment in the proper sequence and with spiritual knowledge <note>original: "religious science"</note> derived from the Hierarchy itself, becoming as much like its own creator as is permissible.

For each of those who have been called into the Hierarchy find their perfection in being led toward Divine initiation <gloss>1</gloss> according to their own specific rank. Even more Divine than all this, as the scriptures <note>original: "oracles"</note> say, is becoming a fellow-worker <gloss>2</gloss> with God and demonstrating the Divine energy dwelling within oneself, making it visible to others as much as possible. It is a rule of the Hierarchy that some are purified while others do the purifying <gloss>3</gloss>; some are enlightened while others enlighten <gloss>4</gloss>; and some are perfected while others lead them toward perfection. In this way, each person will imitate God in their own specific manner. 

The Divine Blessedness is—to speak in human terms—completely free from any internal conflict or inconsistency <gloss>5</gloss>. It is filled with invisible light <gloss>6</gloss> and is perfect <gloss>7</gloss>, needing no further perfection. It is the force that cleanses, illuminates, and perfects. Rather, it is itself holy purification, illumination, and perfection. It exists above purification and above light; it is preeminently perfect, the source of self-perfection, and the cause of every Hierarchy, yet it is raised far above every holy thing. 

It is necessary, then—as I see it—that those being purified should be made entirely perfect so as to be without stain. Those being enlightened should be filled with Divine Light and guided toward the habit and ability of contemplation with total purity of mind. Those being initiated should be separated from the profane and become recipients of that knowledge <note>original: "science"</note> which brings to perfection the holy people who are initiated into the highest mysteries.

Furthermore, those who purify should share their own holiness from their own abundance of purity. Those who enlighten—acting as luminous intelligences whose function is to receive and share light—and who are joyfully filled with...

...holy joy, which they should share—in proportion to their own overflowing light—with those who are worthy of enlightenment.
