<meta>This page transitions from the "Celestial Hierarchy" (the angelic orders) to the "Ecclesiastical Hierarchy" (the earthly church structure). It introduces Dionysius's address to Timothy and defines the divine origin and purpose of the priesthood and the church's sacred mysteries.</meta>

# ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY.

---

## CHAPTER 1 <note>original: *Caput I*</note>

### TO MY FELLOW ELDER <note>original: *Presbyter*</note> TIMOTHY.
### DIONYSIUS THE ELDER.

->*What is the traditional view of the Ecclesiastical Hierarchy, and what is its scope?*<-

We must, then, demonstrate, most devout of devout sons, that ours is a Hierarchy of inspired, divine, and deifying knowledge <note>original: "science"</note>, activity, and perfection. This we will do using the heavenly and most sacred scriptures <note>original: "oracles"</note>—for those who have been initiated into the sacred revelation derived from the hierarchical mysteries and traditions. But take care that you do not treat these most holy things with contempt. Take good heed, and you will then guard the honor of the secret things of God through spiritual and veiled <note>original: "obscure"</note> definitions, carefully guarding them from being shared with or defiled by the uninitiated <note>original: "profane"</note>, and communicating them reverently only to those who are most holy. 

For as the Word of God has taught us—we who feast at His banquet—Jesus Himself is the supremely divine and beyond-being <note>original: *superessential*</note> Mind. He is the Source <note>original: "Head"</note> and Essence, and the most supremely divine power behind every hierarchy, sanctification, and divine activity. He enlightens the blessed beings who are superior to us [the angels] in a manner that is clearer and more direct, making them like His own Light in proportion to their ability to receive it. 

As for ourselves, through the love of beautiful things, He lifts us toward Himself. By gathering together our many differences and forming them into a unified and divine life, He makes us suitable for a sacred calling in both our character and our actions. He Himself grants the power of the divine priesthood. By approaching the holy exercise of the priestly office, we draw closer to the beings above us; we do this by imitating, as much as we are able, their stability and unwavering commitment to holy things. 

Therefore, by looking upward to the blessed and supremely divine glory of Jesus, and reverently gazing upon whatever we are permitted to see, we are enlightened by the knowledge of these visions. In this way, regarding the knowledge of divine mysteries, we shall be able to become both purified and able to purify others—images of Light and coworkers with God, both perfected ourselves and able to bring others to perfection.

<page-num>51</page-num>

# ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY.

<margin>1 See the Epistle to the Trallians. 2 John 1:1.</margin>

What, then, is the Hierarchy of the Angels and Archangels, and the celestial <note>original: "supermundane"</note> Principalities and Authorities, Powers and Lordships, Divine Thrones, or Beings of the same ranks as the Thrones? The Word of God describes these as being near and always around God, and with God. In the Hebrew language, they are called Cherubim and Seraphim <note>original: "Cherubin and Seraphin"</note>. You will find what pertains to the sacred Orders and the divisions of their ranks and Hierarchies in the books we have already written. In those works—written to the best of our ability, if not as perfectly as their dignity deserves—we have followed the Word of God as it describes their Hierarchy in the Holy Scriptures. 

Nevertheless, it is necessary to say this: both that angelic hierarchy and every Hierarchy we are now celebrating possesses the same power throughout all its functions. The Hierarch <term>a high-ranking religious leader, such as a bishop, who oversees sacred rites</term> himself is initiated into Divine things according to his nature, ability, and rank. He is deified <note>original: "deified"; referring to the process of *theosis*, or becoming more like God</note> and, in turn, imparts to his subordinates—according to the fitness of each person—the sacred deification that he himself has received from God. Likewise, subordinates follow their superiors while helping to elevate those below them toward progress. Some go before and, as far as possible, lead others. Each person, as much as they are able, participates in Him who is truly Beautiful, Wise, and Good through this Divine and priestly <note>original: "sacerdotal"</note> harmony.

But the Beings and ranks above us, whom we have already mentioned with reverence, are incorporeal; their Hierarchy is purely spiritual <note>original: "intellectual"</note> and beyond this world. However, we observe that our own hierarchy, in accordance with our human nature, abounds in a wide variety of material symbols. By these symbols, and in proportion to our capacity, we are led through priestly functions toward a unified deification—toward God and Divine Virtue. Because the angels are pure minds, they perceive the truth according to the laws established for them. But we are led toward Divine contemplation through sensory figures <note>original: "sensible figures"; meaning things we can see, touch, or hear</note> as much as is possible for us. 

To speak truly, there is only One whom all God-like beings aspire to reach. Yet they do not partake of this "One" in the same way; rather, the Divine ordinance distributes a suitable inheritance to each. These things have been treated more systematically in the treatise titled *Concerning the Spiritual and the Sensory* <note>original: "Intellectual and Sensible"</note>. But now, I will attempt to describe our own Hierarchy, including its head and its essence, as best I can, invoking Jesus—the Head and Perfection of all Hierarchies. 

Every Hierarchy, according to our solemn tradition, is the complete description of the sacred things falling under it; it is a full summary of the sacred rites of a particular Hierarchy. Our Hierarchy, then, is the systematic account of all the sacred rites included within it. According to this system, the divine Hierarch, once he is initiated, will have within himself the...

<page-num>52</page-num>

# ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY.

...participation in the most sacred things, as the chief of the Hierarchy <note>original Greek: *Ἱεραρχίας ἐπώνυμος*, meaning "the one named after the Hierarchy"</note>. For just as someone who speaks of "Hierarchy" refers to the collective order of all sacred rites, someone who mentions a "Hierarch" <term>a high-ranking religious leader, such as a bishop, who oversees sacred rites</term> denotes an inspired and godly man—one who accurately understands all sacred knowledge, and in whom the entire Hierarchy is completed and recognized in its purity.

The supreme Head of this Hierarchy is the Triad <note>original: "Triad"; the Holy Trinity</note>—the Fountain of Life, the Essence of Goodness, and the single Cause of all things that exist. From Him, through His goodness, all things receive both their existence and their well-being. The will of this supremely Divine blessedness—which is exalted above all, the Three-in-One, the truly existing, and is mysterious to us though known to itself—is the rational salvation of all beings, both those among us and those above us. But that salvation cannot happen in any other way than through the condition that those who are being saved become deified <note>original: "deified"; referring to the process of *theosis*, or becoming more like God</note>. This deification is the process of becoming like God and reaching a state of oneness with Him, as far as is permissible for a created being. 

Now, this is the common goal of every Hierarchy: a devoted love for God and Divine things; a love that is divinely sanctified into oneness with Him. For the sake of this love, there must be a complete and unswerving removal of anything contrary to it. It involves the knowledge of things as they truly are, the vision and clear understanding of sacred truth, and a Divine participation in that uniform perfection—the One itself—as far as possible. It is a banquet of contemplation that intellectually nourishes and deifies everyone who raises themselves toward it.

Let us affirm, then, that the Divine Blessedness—the self-existent Godhead and Source of deification, from whom the deification of the holy comes—bequeathed the Hierarchy through Divine Goodness for the salvation and deification of all rational and intellectual beings. To the heavenly <note>original: "supermundane"</note> and blessed heirs (the angels), this is granted in a more immaterial and intellectual way. God does not move them toward Divine things from the outside; instead, he moves them intellectually, and they are illuminated about the Divine will from within by a pure and heavenly light. 

However, for us, what is given to them simply and uniformly is instead provided through the Divinely transmitted oracles <note>original: "oracles"; referring here to the Holy Scriptures and Divine revelations</note> in a variety of many symbols that we can understand, according to our ability to receive them. For these Divinely transmitted oracles are the very essence of our Hierarchy. We affirm that these oracles are most majestic—including all those given by our inspired founders in the Divinely written letters of the Word of God. Furthermore, we include whatever our leaders have revealed to us from those same holy men through a more spiritual form of instruction—very similar to the Heavenly Hierarchy—passed from mind to mind. This is done through the medium of speech, which is physical, yet it is nevertheless more...

<page-num>53</page-num>

# ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY.

...immaterial—without writing. Nor did the inspired Hierarchs <term>the high-ranking leaders who manage sacred things</term> transmit these things in concepts that were clear to the common people during Divine Service, but rather through sacred symbols. For not everyone is holy, nor, as the scriptures <note>original: "oracles"</note> affirm, does everyone possess knowledge.

It was necessary, then, for the first leaders of our Hierarchy—after they were filled with the sacred gift from the super-essential Godhead <note>original: "super-essential Godhead"; refers to God who exists beyond all human categories of being or essence</note> and sent by Divine Goodness to extend that same gift to those outside—to impart these things to us. As godly men desiring the instruction and deification <note>original: "deification"; the process of becoming like God, also known as *theosis*</note> of those who followed them, they generously shared—through both written and unwritten revelations—heavenly things in physical images. They did this in accordance with sacred commands, representing the simple and unified truths through diverse and complex forms; divine things through human things; immaterial things through material things; and the beyond-essential through earthly things. 

They did not do this merely because of the unholy, who are not permitted even to touch the symbols. They did it because our Hierarchy is, as I said, a kind of symbol adapted to our human condition. We require material objects to help raise our souls from the physical world to the spiritual. However, the true meanings of these symbols have been revealed only to the Divine initiators <note>the bishops or leaders authorized to perform the rites</note>, and it is not permitted to explain them to those who are still being initiated. The Lawgivers of these divinely taught rites deliberately arranged the Hierarchy into well-established and distinct ranks, distributing sacred duties proportionately to each person according to their capacity. 

Therefore, I trust in those sacred promises you made—and it is good to remind you of them—that since every hierarchical doctrine is of supreme importance, you will not entrust these teachings to anyone except those God-like initiators who hold the same rank as yourself. You will persuade them to promise, according to the regulations of our Hierarchy, to handle pure things purely, and to communicate the things of God only to the godly, perfect things to those being perfected, and the most holy things to the holy. I have entrusted this Divine gift to you, in addition to many other hierarchical gifts.

***

->*Concerning those who are being initiated into illumination <note>In the early church, "illumination" often referred to the Sacrament of Baptism.</note>.*<-

We have, then, reverently affirmed that the goal of our Hierarchy is this: becoming like God and achieving oneness with Him, as far as is permitted. But as the Divine scriptures teach, we shall attain this only through love and the religious performance of worshipful...

# ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY.

Commandments. For He says: "He <note>John 14:23</note> who loves Me will keep My commandments, and My Father will love him, and We will come to him and make Our home with him." 

What, then, is the beginning of the religious practice of these most sacred <note>original: "august"</note> commandments? It is the teaching of the sacred and most Divine rebirth <note>original: "regeneration"; John 1:13</note>, which forms a fitness in the habits of our soul to receive the other Divine words and actions <note>John 3:5</note>; it is the preparation of the way for our ascent to heavenly <note>original: "supercelestial"</note> rest. For, as our illustrious leader <note>Referencing the author's teacher, often identified as Hierotheus</note> used to say, the very first movement of the mind toward Divine things is the love of God, but the very earliest approach toward the religious practice of the Divine commandments is the ineffable <note>too great to be expressed in words</note> creation of our existence in God. 

For if our existence in God is a Divine birth <note>original: "engendering"; see Baptismal Offices</note>, then no one could ever know—and certainly not perform—any of the Divine instructions if he had not first begun his existence in God. To speak in human terms, is it not necessary that we should first begin to exist, and then do what is natural for us to do? For he who does not exist at all has neither movement nor even a beginning. But he who actually exists only does or experiences those things suitable to his own condition. 

This, then, I believe is clear. Let us next contemplate the Divine symbols of this birth in God. And I pray that no uninitiated person may approach this sight; for it is not without danger to gaze upon the glorious rays of the sun with weak eyes, nor is it without peril to put our hand to things that are above us. The priesthood of the Law was right to reject Uzziah <note>original: "Osias"; 2 Chronicles 26:16-21. King Uzziah was punished with leprosy for attempting to burn incense in the Temple, a task reserved for priests.</note> because he reached for sacred things; and Korah <note>Numbers 16:1-33. Korah led a rebellion against Moses and Aaron, seeking priestly honors for himself.</note> for reaching for things above his capacity; and Nadab and Abihu <note>Numbers 3:4. The sons of Aaron who were killed for offering "unauthorized fire" before the Lord.</note> because they treated things within their own duty unholily.

### II.—The Mystery of Illumination.

The Hierarch <note>A high-ranking leader or bishop who presides over sacred mysteries</note>, then, desiring <note>1 Timothy 2:4</note> that all people should be saved by becoming like God <note>original: "assimilation towards God"</note> and coming to a recognition of the truth, proclaims the true Gospel to everyone—that God, being compassionate toward people on earth out of His own inherent goodness, deigned to come to us with outstretched arms because of His loving-kindness. And, by union with Himself—like a fire—He makes those who have been joined to Him like Himself, in proportion to their fitness for deification <note>original: *theosis*; the process of being made like God</note>. "For as many as received Him, to them He gave power <note>John 1:12, 13</note> to become sons of God—to those believing in His Name, who were born not of bloods <note>The original Greek text uses "bloods" (plural) to refer to physical descent or lineage.</note>, nor of the will of the flesh, but of God."

...divine gifts, he comes to someone who is already a member of the faithful and persuades this person to act as his guide to the Church leadership <note>original: "Hierarchy"; here referring to the clergy or the Bishop.</note>. He then promises to follow completely the teachings that will be given to him and asks the guide to take charge of his introduction and his entire life from that point on. But the guide—though he religiously longs for the man's salvation—feels a sense of trembling and inadequacy when he measures human weakness against the greatness of the undertaking. Nevertheless, he finally agrees with a willing heart to do what is requested. He takes the man and leads him to the High Priest <note>original: "chief Hierarchy"; the Bishop or Hierarch who presides over the ceremony.</note>. The High Priest receives the man kindly, like a lost sheep upon his shoulders. He admits the two men and, with a silent prayer of thanksgiving and a physical bow, he worships and glorifies the one Author and Finisher of Good <note>See Philippians 2:13</note>, from whom those who are called receive their calling, and through whom those who are saved find their salvation.

He then gathers the entire sacred Order into the holy Choir to participate and rejoice together over the man’s salvation, and also to offer *thanksgiving* to the Divine Goodness. To begin, he chants a specific hymn found in the sacred scriptures <note>original: "oracles"</note>, accompanied by the entire congregation. After this, when he has kissed the holy altar, he approaches the man standing before him and asks him why he has come. The man, out of love for God, confesses his ungodliness <note>original: *atheotēta* (ἀθεότητα); a state of being without God or living in ignorance of Him.</note>, his ignorance of what is truly beautiful, and his own inability to lead a divine life, as his sponsor had instructed him. He asks, through the High Priest's holy mediation, to reach God and divine things. The High Priest testifies to him that his approach must be wholehearted, as he is coming to God, who is all-perfect and without blemish. When he has fully explained the divine way of life to him and asked if he intends to live this way, and after the man has promised to do so, the High Priest places his right hand on the man's head. Once he has marked him with a seal <note>likely the Sign of the Cross</note>, he commands the priests to register both the man and his sponsor.

When they have enrolled the names, the High Priest offers a holy prayer. Once the entire Church has finished this prayer with him, he removes the man's sandals and clothing with the help of the Deacons. Then, after placing the man facing the west while he gestures with his hands in rejection toward that direction, he commands him three times to blow in mockery at Satan <note>This refers to "exsufflation," an ancient ritual of breathing or blowing to symbolize the casting out of demons.</note> and, further, to speak the words of renunciation. After witnessing this threefold renunciation, the High Priest turns him toward the east. Once the man has confessed his faith three times, looked up to heaven, and raised his hands toward it, the High Priest commands him to be subject to Christ and to all the divinely transmitted words of God. When the man has done this, the High Priest witnesses his threefold confession once again, and then...

<meta>This page continues the description of the ritual of baptism, moving from the preparatory anointing and the blessing of the water to the immersion itself, the clothing of the newly baptized, and the beginning of the spiritual "Contemplation" that explains the symbolic meaning of these acts.</meta>

<page-num>56</page-num>

<header>ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY.</header>

After the candidate has made his confession and prayer, the Hierarch <note>The High Priest or Bishop presiding over the ceremony.</note> gives thanks and lays his hand upon him. When the Deacons have entirely undressed the candidate, the Priests bring the holy oil of the chrism <note>A consecrated oil used for anointing.</note>. Then the Hierarch begins the anointing by sealing the candidate with the sign of the cross three times. He then assigns the man to the Priests to have his whole body anointed, while he himself moves to the baptismal font—the "mother of spiritual adoption." He dedicates the water within it through sacred prayers and consecrates it by pouring the holy myrrh into it three times in the shape of a cross. After he has three times invoked the holy melody of the Divinely inspired Prophets—just as he did when adding the myrrh—he orders the man to be brought forward. 

When one of the Priests has announced the names of the candidate and his sponsor from the register, the Priests lead the man through the water to the Hierarch. Then the Hierarch, standing above him, as the Priests again call out the name of the initiate within the water, dips him three times. At each immersion and emersion of the initiate, the Hierarch invokes the Threefold Personhood <note>original: τρισσὴν ὑπόστασιν (trissēn hypostasin); the "threefold subsistence" or the Three Persons of the Trinity. See Hebrews 1:3.</note> of the Divine Blessedness. The Priests then take the man and entrust him to his sponsor and guide.

When the sponsor and Priests together have dressed the initiate in appropriate clothing, they lead him back to the Hierarch. The Hierarch then seals the man with the most holy myrrh and declares him from that point on a participant in the most sacred Eucharist <note>The sacrament of Holy Communion.</note>.

When the Hierarch has finished these rites, he moves from these secondary actions back to the contemplation of primary truths. He never turns his attention to any office other than his own; rather, by gradually advancing in Divine matters, he is always and persistently aligning himself under the banner of the supreme Divine Spirit.

### III.—*Contemplation.*

This initiation of the holy birth in God uses symbols that are neither unbecoming nor irreverent. They are not merely physical images, but mysteries of a contemplation worthy of God, using natural images that humans can understand. Even if we passed over the deeper spiritual account of these religious ceremonies in silence, their Divine institution would still be persuasive; the ritual diligently encourages the candidate toward a good life. It announces to him, in a physical form, his purification from every kind of evil through a virtuous and Divine life, represented by the physical cleansing of the water.

<header>ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY.</header>

the use of water. Even if the symbolic teaching of these religious rites contained nothing more divine, it would still, I believe, have religious value. It introduces a discipline for a well-regulated life by suggesting, through the physical purification of the body with water, a complete cleansing from an evil life. For those not yet fully initiated, let this be an introductory guidance for the soul—rightfully separating sacred and unified things from the clutter of the world, and restoring our internal harmony to its proper order by degrees. 

But we, who have risen by the proper stages to the contemplation of the highest mysteries and have been religiously taught their hidden meanings, will recognize the patterns of which these rites are the copies, and the invisible things of which they are the likenesses. As is clearly shown in the treatise *Concerning the Intellectual and the Sensory* <note>original: "Intelligible and Sensible." This refers to the Neoplatonic distinction between the spiritual world (*intelligible*), understood by the mind, and the physical world (*sensible*), perceived by the senses.</note>, physical sacred objects are representations of spiritual realities to which they lead and show the way. Conversely, spiritual realities are the original patterns and explanations for the sacred things we perceive with our senses. 

Let us affirm, then, that the goodness of the Divine Blessedness—God—is always the same in its nature and purpose, generously unfolding the kind rays of its own light upon all spiritual eyes. There are two ways this light might be lost. First, through our own free will, we might turn away from this spiritual light by closing our capacity for enlightenment out of a love for evil. In doing so, we separate ourselves from the light that is present to us—not because the light has turned away, but because it continues to shine even as we shut ourselves in darkness, and it generously follows even those who move away from it. Second, a person might overstep the limits of the vision they have been given and rashly attempt to stare at rays of light that are far beyond their spiritual sight. In this case, the light itself is simply performing its proper function, but the person, being imperfect and approaching the perfect, fails to reach things that do not fit their condition. By foolishly ignoring their own limits, they fail through their own fault.

But as I said, the Divine Light is always offered for kind purposes to all spiritual visions. It is possible for those visions to grasp the light when it is present, as it is always ready to distribute its gift in a manner worthy of the Deity. The divine Hierarch <note>The Bishop or high-ranking leader of the church hierarchy.</note> is formed in the image of this Light. He reveals the luminous rays of his own divine teaching to everyone without reservation. Following God’s example, he is most ready to enlighten anyone who comes to him, never using malice or unholy anger because of their past sins or excesses. Instead, following the example of God, he always enlightens those who approach him in a way that fits his role as a Hierarch—with appropriateness, order, and in proportion to each person's readiness for divine things.

However, since the Deity is the Origin of that sacred order within which the holy Minds <note>Referring to angels or perfected human souls.</note> who know themselves move, he—

Anyone who returns to a true view of nature will see their own true self as they were originally created, and will receive this as the first sacred gift of their restoration to the light. Now, once a person has looked upon their own condition with unbiased eyes, they will depart from the dark recesses of ignorance. However, while still uninitiated, they will not immediately desire the most perfect Unity and participation in God of their own accord. Instead, they will advance little by little—moving from their present state to things further along, and from those to the very highest things. Once they have been perfected, they will be led in an orderly and heavenly manner to the supreme divine height.

The modesty of the person approaching the Hierarchy is an image of this good order. They recognize their own internal condition by providing a sponsor to act as a leader in their approach. The Divine Blessedness <note>original: "Divine Blessedness." This refers to God as the source of all happiness and sanctity.</note> receives the person led in this way into communion with itself. It imparts its own light to him as a kind of sign, making him a godly sharer in the inheritance and sacred order of holy people. The Hierarchical seal given to the convert <note>original: "proselyte"</note> is a sacred symbol of this. Furthermore, the priests' registry, acting as a record of salvation, lists him among those who are in a state of salvation. It places both his sponsor and himself in the sacred memorials—the initiate as a true lover and follower of a godly guide on the life-giving journey toward truth, and the sponsor as an unerring guide leading his newcomer <note>original: "neophyte"</note> toward divine instructions.

But it is not possible to hold onto qualities that are completely opposed to one another. A man who has entered into fellowship with the One <note>original: "the One." A Neoplatonic term for God, emphasizing His absolute unity.</note> cannot lead a divided life if he intends to cling to a firm participation in that Unity. Instead, he must be unwavering and persistent in resisting anything that separates him from that single-minded focus. This is what the teaching of the symbols reverently suggests by stripping the newcomer of his former life, figuratively unfastening all the habits of that life to their very ends. 

The ritual places him naked and barefoot, facing West. By turning his hands away, he rejects any participation in dark evil. It is as if he is breathing out the mismatched habits he had acquired and proclaiming his total renunciation of everything contrary to the divine likeness. When the man has thus become completely firm and separate from evil, the guide leads him toward the East. This clearly declares that his position and restoration will be found in the Divine Light, in total separation from evil. There, he makes sacred promises to lean entirely toward the One, as a man who has acquired the image of the One through a love of the truth. 

As I believe is quite evident to those experienced in Hierarchical matters, spiritual people acquire the unchangeable nature of a Godlike character through continuous and persistent struggles toward unity, and by the entire destruction and annihilation of everything contrary to it. For it is necessary that a...

<meta>This page continues the "Contemplation" (spiritual explanation) of the baptismal rite, moving from the initiate's initial approach to the symbolic meaning of the anointing and the water of baptism as a mystical death and resurrection.</meta>

<page-num>59</page-num>

## ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY.

A person should not only turn away from every kind of evil, but must be bravely steadfast and always disdainful toward any harmful submission to it. Nor must they ever become neglectful in their sacred love of the truth. Instead, they must use all their power to persistently and perpetually aspire toward it, always religiously pursuing their upward course to the more perfect mysteries of the supreme Godhead.

Now, you can see the clear images of these concepts in the religious rites performed by the Hierarch <note>original: "Hierarch." In this context, it refers to the Bishop who presides over the ceremony.</note>. For the God-like Hierarch starts with the holy anointing, but the Priests under him complete the divine service of the Chrism <note>original: "Chrism." A consecrated oil used in baptism and other sacraments to signify the gift of the Holy Spirit.</note>. They summon the person being initiated to the holy contests in which he is placed under Christ as the Umpire. Since He is God, He is the Creator of the laws of this contest; as He is wise, He established its rules; and as He is noble and generous, He determined the rewards suitable for the victors.<sup>1</sup> This is even more divine because, in His generosity, He entered the lists with them, struggling on behalf of their freedom<sup>2</sup> and victory against the power of death<sup>3</sup> and destruction<sup>4</sup>. Therefore, the person being initiated will enter these contests as if they were the contests of a rejoicing God. He will remain fixed in the regulations of the Wise One and will struggle according to them without breaking the rules, holding fast to the hope of beautiful rewards, since he is enrolled under a good Lord and Leader of the contest. When he has followed in the divine footsteps of the first of Athletes <note>The "first of Athletes" refers to Christ, who is depicted here as the champion and model for the believer’s spiritual struggle.</note> in terms of goodness, and has overthrown—through his struggles following the divine example—the energies and impulses opposed to his deification <note>original: "deification." This refers to the process of *theosis*, or becoming like God through grace.</note>, he dies with Christ to sin in his baptism (speaking mystically).

But consider this attentively, I pray, and see how appropriately the holy symbols are presented. For us, death is not an annihilation of our being (as some others believe), but rather the separation of things that were united, leading to our becoming invisible. The soul becomes invisible by being deprived of the body, and the body, being buried in the earth as one of its various changes, becomes invisible to human sight. Appropriately, then, the total covering by water is used as an image of death and the darkness of the tomb. The symbolic teaching reveals that the man baptized according to religious rites imitates—as far as divine imitation is allowed for a human—the supremely divine death of the life-giving Jesus. He spent three days and three nights in the tomb,<sup>5</sup> <note>original: "τριημερονύκτου ταφῆς" (triēmeronyktou taphēs), meaning "a burial of three days and nights."</note> in whom, according to the mystical and secret teaching of the sacred text, the Prince of this world <note>A biblical title for Satan.</note> found nothing.<sup>6</sup>

Next, they throw garments, white as light, over the initiated person.

For through his courageous and God-like detachment <note>original: "insensibility." For the holy perfection of the Divine birth <note>original: "Divine birth." A reference to the sacrament of Baptism.</note> unites those who have been perfected with the supreme Divine Spirit. Now, as for the spiritual indwelling that brings perfection and a good scent—which is beyond words—I leave that to the spiritual awareness of those deemed worthy of the sacred and deifying participation of the Holy Spirit within their own minds.

At the conclusion of everything, the Hierarch <note>original: "Hierarch." A high-ranking leader of the church hierarchy, such as a bishop.</note> invites the newly perfected person to the most Holy Eucharist and grants him communion in these most perfecting mysteries.

### CHAPTER III. <note>original: "CAPUT III."</note>

->*Concerning those who are being initiated in the Synaxis <note>original: "Synaxis." A Greek term meaning "gathering" or "assembly," specifically referring to the Eucharistic liturgy.</note>.*<-

Courage, then; because we have mentioned the Eucharist, we must not pass over it to celebrate any other hierarchical function first. For according to our illustrious Leader <note>Likely referring to Dionysius’s mentor, Hierotheus, or to St. Paul.</note>, it is the "initiation of initiations." We must first provide a divine description of this sacrament, based on the inspired and sacred science of the scriptures <note>original: "oracles"</note>, and then be guided by the supreme Divine Spirit toward its sacred contemplation. 

First, let us reverently consider this: why is the term "Communion and Synaxis" uniquely applied to this rite, even though it describes what is common to all other hierarchical initiations? For every consecrating act gathers our fragmented lives into a unified deification <note>original: "deification." The process of being made like God (theosis).</note> and grants us communion and oneness with the One, by bringing our many distractions into a God-like harmony. 

However, we affirm that the perfection found in the other hierarchical symbols actually flows from the divine and perfecting gifts of this Sacrament. For it almost never happens that any hierarchical initiation is celebrated without the most Divine Eucharist presiding over the rites. The Eucharist divinely achieves the gathering of the initiate into the One and completes his communion with God through the divinely transmitted gift of these perfecting mysteries. Therefore, if any of the hierarchical initiations fail to complete our communion and our gathering to the One—being incomplete in themselves—then their status as an "initiation" is essentially lost because of that lack. For as the sharing of the supreme...

Since the divine mysteries are the completion and peak of every initiation for the person being initiated, the hierarchical wisdom chose a name for it based on the truth of what it accomplishes. For instance, consider the holy initiation of the Divine birth <note>original: "Divine birth." A common term in this text for the sacrament of Baptism.</note>. Since it imparts the "first-Light" and is the supreme head of all divine illuminations, we celebrate its true name based on the enlightenment it confers. For although it is common to all hierarchical functions to give the gift of sacred light to those being initiated, this specific rite gave me the power of "first sight." Through its primary light, I was led to see the other religious rites. Having said this, let us minutely investigate and examine, as is appropriate for the Hierarchy, the perfect administration and contemplation of this most holy initiation.

### II.—The *Mystery*¹ of Synaxis <note>original: "Synaxis." A Greek term meaning "assembly" or "gathering," specifically referring here to the Eucharistic liturgy.</note>, that is, Communion.

The <term>Hierarch</term> <note>original: "Hierarch." The high-ranking leader of the church service, usually a bishop.</note>, having completed a reverent prayer near the Divine Altar, begins with the incense and proceeds to cense every part of the sacred enclosure. He then returns to the Divine Altar and begins the sacred chanting of the Psalms, with the whole ecclesiastical Order chanting the sacred language of the Psalter with him. Next follows the reading of the Holy Scriptures by the <term>Leitourgoi</term> <note>original: "Leitourgoi." Ministers or deacons who assist in the liturgy.</note>. After these readings, the <term>catechumens</term> <note>original: "catechumens." Students of the faith who have not yet been baptized.</note> leave the sacred enclosure, along with the <term>energumenoi</term> <note>original: "energumenoi." Individuals believed to be troubled or possessed by spirits.</note> and the <term>penitents</term> <note>original: "penitents." Members of the church undergoing a period of correction for sins.</note>. 

However, those who are deemed worthy to see and participate in the Divine Mysteries remain. Of the <term>Leitourgoi</term>, some stand near the closed gates of the sanctuary, while others perform various duties of their specific office. Then, chosen members of the diaconal Order (the deacons), along with the Priests, place the sacred Bread and the Cup of Blessing upon the Divine Altar while the universal song² of praise is sung by the entire body of the Church. Afterward, the Divine <term>Hierarch</term> offers a sacred prayer and proclaims the "Holy Peace" to everyone. 

When everyone has greeted each other, the mystical proclamation of the holy tablets <note>original: "tablets." Likely the "diptychs," or lists of names of the living and the dead to be remembered in prayer.</note> is performed. After the <term>Hierarch</term> and the Priests have washed their hands in water, the <term>Hierarch</term> stands in the center of the Divine Altar, surrounded only by the chosen Deacons and the Priests. When the <term>Hierarch</term> has commemorated the sacred works of God, he consecrates the most divine gifts. He brings the mysteries into view through the symbols that have been reverently set out.³ When he has shown these free gifts of God’s work, he himself participates in the sacred communion and then turns to exhort the others. When he has received and distributed the—

### III.—*Contemplation.*

After the images, I come now, most excellent son, in due order and reverence, to the Divine reality of the archetypes. To those still being initiated, I would say this for the harmonious guidance of their souls: the varied and sacred composition of the symbols provides spiritual instruction even to them, even when it is only presented superficially. For the most sacred chants and readings of the oracles <note>original: "oracles." Here referring to the Holy Scriptures.</note> teach them the discipline of a virtuous life and, what is more than this, complete purification from destructive evil.

But the most Divine, general, and peaceful distribution of the same Bread and Cup requires of them a fellowship in character as well as a fellowship in food. This recalls to their memory the most Divine Supper and the primary symbol <note>original: "arch-symbol."</note> of the rites performed. In accordance with this, the Founder of the symbols himself rightly excludes anyone who joined the feast of heavenly things without piety,¹ and in a manner unlike himself. He teaches at once, clearly and Divinely, that approaching the Divine mysteries with a sincere mind grants those who draw near a participation in a gift similar to their own character. 

Let us, then, as I said, leave behind these things—beautifully depicted upon the entryway <term>propylea</term> <note>original: "propylea." The porch or gateway leading to the inner sanctuary.</note> of the innermost shrine—as they are sufficient for those who are not yet ready for contemplation. Let us proceed from the effects to the cause; and then, with Jesus lighting the way, let us view our holy <term>Synaxis</term> <note>original: "Synaxis." The Greek term for the gathering or assembly for Holy Communion.</note> and the beautiful contemplation of the spiritual mysteries, which reveals the blessed beauty of the archetypes. Oh, most Divine and holy initiation, uncover the folds of the dark mysteries that wrap you in symbols! Be manifest to us in your bright glory, and fill our intellectual visions with unique and clear light.

In my opinion, we must pass within the All-Holy Mysteries after we have laid bare the spiritual meaning of the first images. We do this to gaze upon their God-like beauty and contemplate with a godly mind the Hierarch as he travels with fragrance from the Divine Altar to the furthest bounds of the holy place and is, at the end, restored to the same spot. For the Blessedness, which is supremely Divine above all, although through...

Divine goodness reaches out to share itself with those who participate in it, yet it never leaves its essential, unmoved, and steady state.

Furthermore, it grants divine illumination to all according to their ability to receive it; it remains always centered within itself and is never changed from its own true identity. In the same way, the divine initiation of the <term>Synaxis</term> <note>original: "Synaxis." A Greek term for a church assembly or the service of Holy Communion.</note>—though it has a single, simple, and unified origin—is expanded into a holy variety of symbols out of love for humanity. It moves through the entire range of divine imagery, yet it is consistently gathered back from these symbols into its own true unity, unifying those who are reverently led toward it. In this same divine manner, the <term>Hierarch</term> <note>A high-ranking religious leader, such as a bishop.</note> kindly shares his unique knowledge of the hierarchy with his subordinates by using various divine mysteries <note>original: "enigmas." Used here to mean sacred symbols or riddles that reveal divine truth.</note>. Yet, because he is unconstrained and cannot be limited by lesser things, he returns to his own supreme dignity without losing anything.

When he has entered into his own spiritual unity, he clearly sees the consistent reasons behind the religious rites. While his goal in reaching out to others is to help them, his ultimate aim is the even more divine return to primary truths. The chanting of the *Psalms*, which is essential to almost all the mysteries of the hierarchy, was certainly not going to be left out of this, the most important mystery of all. 

For all holy and inspired *Scriptures* set forth for those ready for <term>deification</term> <note>The process of being made more like God or entering into union with Him.</note> various truths: 
* the origin and divine order of created things; 
* the priesthood and the laws of the community; 
* the distribution and inheritance of the land of God’s people; 
* the wisdom of the holy judges, wise kings, or inspired priests; 
* the philosophy of ancient men who were unshakeable in enduring countless and heavy trials; 
* the treasures of wisdom for living well; 
* songs or inspired images of divine love; 
* prophetic visions of the future; 
* the works of God-made-man performed by Jesus; 
* the leadership and sacred doctrines of His Apostles, given by God so we might imitate Him; 
* the hidden and mystical visions of the beloved and divine disciple; 
* or the heavenly theology of Jesus. 

These are all included in the holy and divine instructions of the initiation ceremonies. However, the sacred performance of the divine songs—which celebrate God's words and works and praise the holy actions of godly men—creates a universal narrative hymn of divine things; which

<meta>This page continues the "Contemplation" section on the religious rites of the Church, specifically focusing on how the chanting of Psalms and the reading of Scripture prepare the soul for higher mysteries.</meta>

<header>ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY.</header>
<page-num>64</page-num>

...creates in those who reverently recite it a habit suitable for receiving and sharing every hierarchical mystery.

When the universal melody of the holy Psalms has harmonized our souls' spiritual state to the rites about to be celebrated—and when the unison of these divine songs, acting as one concordant chorus of divine things, has established harmony toward God, within ourselves <note>original: "ourselves," referring to internal psychological harmony.</note>, and with one another—then the complex and hidden meanings in the spiritual language of the mystical Psalms are expanded and clarified. This is done through the most holy readings of the inspired scriptures, which provide fuller and more distinct accounts and narratives. Anyone who devoutly contemplates these readings will perceive a single, unified inspiration, as if moved by the one, supremely Divine Spirit. 

Naturally, in the history of salvation, the New Covenant is proclaimed after the more ancient tradition <note>original: "more ancient tradition," referring to the Law of the Old Testament.</note>. This inspired order of the Hierarchy teaches us this: that the Old Covenant affirmed that the divine works of Jesus were to come, while the New Covenant actually accomplished them. And while the former described the truth in symbols, the latter showed the truth as a present reality. For the actual accomplishment of these works established the truth of the earlier predictions; the work of God is the fulfillment of the Word of God.

Those who have no desire for these sacred initiations do not even recognize their symbols. They boldly reject the saving revelation of spiritual rebirth, fulfilling to their own ruin the words, "I have no desire to know Your ways" <note>Job 21:14.</note>.

Now, the custom of the holy Hierarchy allows the <term>catechumens</term> <note>Those receiving instruction in the faith but not yet baptized.</note> and the <term>penitents</term> <note>Those undergoing a period of public repentance for sins before being readmitted to full communion.</note> to hear the sacred chanting of the Psalms and the inspired reading of the most Holy Scriptures. However, it does not invite them to the religious services and contemplations that follow, which are reserved for the complete visions of those who have been fully initiated. For the godlike Hierarchy is defined by a holy justice; it distributes a share of divine things to each person according to what is beneficial and appropriate, in proper measure and at the right time. 

The lowest rank is assigned to the catechumens. They do not yet participate in or receive instruction regarding any of the hierarchical functions. They do not yet even possess a "divine existence" through spiritual rebirth, but are being led toward that birth by the instructions of the Church fathers. They are being shaped by life-giving teachings toward the blessed goal of receiving their first true life and first light through birth in God <note>A reference to the Sacrament of Baptism.</note>. Just as physical children, if they were delivered prematurely while still immature and unformed, would fall to the earth without life and without light—like those who are stillborn—so no one in their right mind would say, based on what they saw, that they...

<meta>This page continues the "Contemplation" section from the previous page, moving from the reading of the scriptures to the dismissal of those not yet ready to witness the high mysteries of the Eucharist.</meta>

<header>ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY.</header>
<page-num>65</page-num>

Just as infants are brought to the light when they are released from the darkness of the womb—for medical authorities learned in the body's functions would say that light operates on things capable of receiving light—so also the sacred science <note>original: "science," referring here to the systematic knowledge of theology and liturgy.</note> of religious rites brings initiates to birth through the preparatory nourishment of life-giving instructions. 

When it has prepared them for their Divine Birth, it grants them a saving participation in the luminous and perfecting mysteries in their proper order. However, for the present, it separates those who are perfect from those who are imperfect, maintaining the proper order of divine things and the spiritual birth and life of the <term>catechumens</term> <note>individuals receiving basic instruction in the faith before baptism</note> according to the God-given sequence of the sacred rites.

As for the group of the <term>energumenoi</term> <note>original Greek: "those worked upon" by spirits; the possessed.</note>, their state is indeed unholy, yet they rank second above the <term>catechumens</term>, who are at the lowest level. I do not believe they are on the same level as those who are entirely uninstructed and have had no part in the divine initiations. This is because they have already shared in some of the most holy functions, even though they are still held in chains by opposing qualities, whether through enchantments or terrors. Therefore, the sight and participation in the holy mysteries is withdrawn from them, and very properly so. 

Consider the truly godly man, the worthy partaker of the divine mysteries. To the best of his ability, he is raised to the very summit of the divine likeness through complete and perfect <term>deification</term> <note>the process of being made like God or union with God.</note>. He does not follow the desires of the flesh beyond what is strictly necessary for nature, and even then, he avoids anything superfluous. At the same time, he becomes both a temple and a follower of the supremely Divine Spirit through this high state of <term>deification</term>, striving to add like to like. 

Such a person would not be possessed by opposing phantoms or fears; instead, he would laugh them to scorn. When they approach, he would put them to flight, acting with power rather than just trying to persuade them. Furthermore, because of his character—which is both passionless and indomitable—he would even serve as a physician to others who are possessed.

But I go further; indeed, I know for certain that the clear judgment of the <term>Hierarchy</term> <note>the organized body of church government and its sacred orders</note> is that those who abandon a God-like life are even more "possessed." By turning away from things that truly exist—from undying possessions and everlasting pleasures—they become of one mind and condition with destructive demons. They pursue base follies that destroy them, chasing after earthly instability, vexed by many passions and corrupting pleasures. They seek comfort in things foreign to their nature—things that are not real but only appear to be. Therefore, the <term>Deacon</term>, using his discriminating authority, excludes these individuals first and most appropriately. For it is not permitted to...

<meta>This page continues the "Contemplation" section regarding the dismissal of various groups from the Eucharist. It details the groups excluded from witnessing the mysteries—the uninitiated, the apostates, the weak-willed, and the impure—before describing the "Hierarchical thanksgiving" (the Creed or Preface) performed by those who remain.</meta>

<header>ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY.</header>
<page-num>66</page-num>

It allows them no part in any other holy function except the teaching of the scriptures <note>original: "oracles"</note>, intended to turn them toward better things. For if the Divine Service of the heavenly mysteries excludes from its view the penitents and those who have already approached it—not permitting anyone who is not completely perfect to draw near—it proclaims this in all sincerity: "I am invisible and incommunicable to those who are in any respect imperfectly weak regarding the attainment of the highest degree of the Divine Likeness." (For that most impartial voice also scares away those who cannot be associated with the worthy partakers of the Divine mysteries). How much more, then, will the multitude of those who are under the sway of their passions be unholy and alienated from every view of, and participation in, the holy mysteries? 

First, the uninstructed and those uninitiated in the mysteries are put outside the Divine Temple and the holy service that is above their capacity. With them go the apostates from the religious life. Next are those who, through a lack of fortitude <note>original: "manliness"</note>, are easily affected by the fears and fancies of opposing influences, as they have not yet reached the stability and activity of a God-like condition through a persistent and indomitable inclination toward godliness. In addition to these are those who have indeed separated from a life of sin but have not yet been cleansed from its imaginations by a godly and pure habit and love. And next are those who are not altogether uniform in spirit and—to use an expression of the Law—are not "entirely without spot and blemish." 

Then the sacred ministers of sacred things, who love to gaze upon sacred rites, contemplate in all purity the most holy mystery. They celebrate in a universal Hymn of Praise the Author and Giver of all good, from whom the mysteries were revealed to us as images of salvation which divinely work the sacred deification <note>original: "theosis"</note> of the initiated. Now, some call that hymn a Hymn of Praise, others call it the symbol of worship, but others—as I think, more Divinely—call it a Hierarchical thanksgiving, as it gives a summary of the holy gifts that come to us from God. For it seems to me to be a systematic record <gloss>1</gloss> <note>Appendix: Liturgy of Dionysius.</note> of all the works of God on our behalf, celebrated in song. After it has established our being and life after the pattern of Divine goodness, and molded the Divine likeness within ourselves according to beautiful archetypes, it places us in possession of a more Divine condition and elevation. But when it witnessed the lack of Divine gifts that came upon us through our own heedlessness, it deigned to call us back to our original condition by the restoration of our blessings. By the complete assumption of our nature <note>referring to the Incarnation</note>, He made good the most perfect impartation of His own, and thus gave us a participation in God and Divine things.

When the Divine goodness has thus been religiously celebrated,

<meta>This page continues the description of the Divine Liturgy (the Eucharist), moving from the dismissal of those unfit to participate to the beginning of the core mysteries. It focuses on the themes of unity and purity as the Hierarch (the bishop or chief priest) prepares to approach the altar.</meta>

<header>ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY.</header>
<page-num>67</page-num>

...the holy Bread is presented, covered by a veil, and likewise the Cup of Blessing. The most Divine salutation <note>The "kiss of peace," a ritual sign of fellowship and reconciliation among the congregation.</note> is devoutly performed, followed by the instructive and heavenly proclamation of the holy names written on the tablets <note>original: "tablets"; these are the diptychs, or lists of names of the living and the departed to be remembered in prayer.</note>. For it is not possible for us to be gathered together into the One, or to share in the peaceful unity of the One, when we are divided among ourselves. If we are to be enlightened by the contemplation and knowledge of the One and joined in a consistent and Divine harmony, we must not allow ourselves to fall into conflicting passions <note>original: "lusts"</note>. These passions create earthly enmities—envious and angry—against what is natural to our souls. In my opinion, this unified and undivided life is established through the Divine Service of the greeting of peace; it joins like to like and separates Divine and unified visions from things that are divided. 

The reading of the holy tablets after the greeting proclaims the names of those who have lived holy lives and reached the end of a virtuous journey without stumbling. This reading encourages us and leads us toward their blessed state and Divine inheritance by following their example. Furthermore, it announces that they are truly living, for as the Word of God says, they are “not dead, but have passed from death to a most divine life.”<sup>1</sup> But take note: they are recorded in the holy memorials not because Divine memory is limited like human memory—needing a written list—but, to speak with reverence toward God, they are kept within the cherished and unfailing knowledge that God has of those who have been perfected in His likeness. For “the Lord knows those who are His,” say the scriptures <note>original: "oracles"</note>,<sup>2</sup> and “precious in the sight of the Lord is the death of His saints.”<sup>3</sup> In this context, the “death of saints” refers to their perfection in holiness. 

Keep this sacred fact in mind: after the venerable symbols representing Christ <note>The bread and wine.</note> are placed on the Divine Altar, the reading of the register of holy persons follows immediately. This signifies that they are inseparably joined to His heavenly and sacred Oneness. Once these things have been performed according to the established rules, the Hierarch <note>The chief priest or bishop.</note>, standing before the most holy symbols, washes his hands with water along with the respected order of the priests. This is because, as the scriptures testify, when a person has been washed, they need no other washing except for their hands <note>original: "extremities"; a reference to Jesus washing the feet of the disciples in John 13:10.</note>.<sup>4</sup> Through this final cleansing, performed in a state of being like God, the Hierarch can approach secondary matters while remaining free and focused. By turning himself solely toward the One, he can complete his service without spot or blemish, preserving the full and complete likeness of God. There was even a sacred washbasin <note>original: "laver"</note> for this purpose, as we have mentioned, in the priestly system of the Old Testament <note>original: "Hierarchy of the Law"</note>.<sup>5</sup>

<meta>This page continues the explanation of the liturgy, specifically the symbolic meaning behind the washing of the hands and the theological history of humanity's fall and God's intervention.</meta>

<header>ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY.</header>
<page-num>68</page-num>

The washing of the hands by the <term>Hierarch</term> <note>The bishop or chief priest presiding over the ceremony.</note> and the priests suggests this: those who approach this most sacred service must be purified in their very souls, even in their most distant thoughts. They must approach, as much as possible, by becoming like the Divine itself. In this way, they will reflect the glory of the Divine revelations more clearly, just as heavenly rays pass through bright, clean mirrors more thoroughly and brilliantly.

Furthermore, the washing of the <term>Hierarch</term> and the priests—down to their very fingertips—takes place before the most holy symbols <note>The bread and wine of the Eucharist.</note>, as if in the presence of Christ. He sees our innermost thoughts, and this complete purification is required under His watchful eye and His just, unwavering judgment. Thus, the <term>Hierarch</term> becomes one with divine things. When he has performed these holy acts of God, he consecrates the most divine symbols and presents them to be seen while hymns of praise are sung.

We will now explain, to the best of our ability, the "works of God" we have mentioned. We are not worthy to fully praise them, much less to accurately understand and reveal their mysteries to others. However, we will describe what has been sung and religiously performed by holy <term>Hierarchs</term> based on the <term>Oracles</term> <note>original: "oracles"; referring to the Holy Scriptures.</note>, as far as we are permitted, calling upon their inspiration for help. 

Immediately after human nature had carelessly fallen from God’s blessings, it was seized by a life troubled by many passions and, eventually, a destructive death. As a natural result, our harmful departure from true goodness and the breaking of the sacred Law given in Paradise delivered humanity—once restless under God’s life-giving guidance <note>original: "yoke"</note>—to its own downward urges and the deceptive, hostile traps of the adversary <note>The devil or forces of evil.</note>. Thus, humanity sadly traded eternal life for mortality. 

Because the human race began to originate from these corrupted generations, our end naturally matched our beginning. Since mankind had willingly abandoned a life led by the Divine, they were pulled to the opposite extreme: the instability of many passions. While wandering from the straight path leading to the true God and being subjected to a destructive and evil variety of influences, they naturally forgot their purpose. They ended up worshipping not gods or friends, but enemies. When these enemies treated the human race cruelly, humanity was in danger of being completely destroyed. 

But the limitless love of the supreme Divine goodness toward man did not, in its benevolence, withdraw its spontaneous care. Instead, it took upon itself a real participation in our entire nature, yet without sin,<sup>1</sup> and having made itself one...

<meta>This page continues the theological explanation of the Incarnation and how the liturgical service of the Eucharist (the Divine Service) mirrors Christ's work in restoring humanity from its fallen state to a state of communion with God.</meta>

<header>ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY.</header>
<page-num>69</page-num>

Dealing with our weakness, while maintaining its own nature entirely pure and without flaw, the Divine gave us—as members of the same family—fellowship with itself from that point forward and declared us partakers of its own blessings. As the secret teaching holds, it broke the power $^1$ of the rebellious discord <note>original: "diversity"; referring to the state of being separated from God's unity.</note> that stood against us. This was not done by force, as if simply having the upper hand, but according to the Divine saying, mystically passed down to us, it was done "in judgment and righteousness." $^2$

He then kindly changed our condition to its opposite. He filled the darkness of our minds with perfect and most Divine light, and decorated our souls—which had lost his image—with Godlike beauty. He freed the dwelling place <term>dwelling place</term> <note>original: "tabernacle"; a reference to the human body or soul as a temporary home.</note> $^3$ of our souls, which was on the verge of collapsing, from condemned passions and destructive stains through a perfect salvation. He showed us a heavenly <term>heavenly</term> <note>original: "supermundane"; literally "above the world."</note> elevation and a Divine way of life by making us like his own holiness, as far as is permitted. 

But how could we ever imitate God unless the memory of his most holy works was constantly renewed through the holy teaching and service of the Hierarchy? We do this, then, as the Scriptures <note>original: "oracles"</note> say, "in remembrance of him." $^4$ Therefore, the Divine <term>Hierarch</term> <note>The bishop or chief priest presiding over the liturgy.</note>, standing before the Divine Altar, celebrates the holy works of God which come from the godly foresight of Jesus on our behalf—works he completed for the salvation of our race by the good pleasure of the most Holy Father and in the Holy Spirit, as the Divine Word <note>referring to the Bible</note> affirms. $^5$ 

When the <term>Hierarch</term> has celebrated their majesty and gazed with his mind's eye upon these spiritual truths, he proceeds to the Divine service that symbolizes them—a service handed down from God. Therefore, after the sacred praise of the works of God, he expresses his unworthiness <note>original: "deprecates"; a formal prayer of humility.</note> for a service that is far beyond what he deserves. First, he reverently says aloud, "You have said, 'Do this in remembrance of me.'" $^6$ Then, $^7$ having asked to become worthy <term>worthy</term> <note>original: "meet"</note> for this holy office of imitating the Divine, he asks to consecrate the Divine symbols <note>The bread and wine.</note> by being made like Christ himself, and to distribute them in perfect holiness. He prays that those who partake of these holy mysteries may receive them with devotion. 

He then consecrates the most Divine symbols and brings the mysteries into view through the reverent display of the symbols while hymns of praise are sung. For when he has uncovered the bread (which was covered and whole) and broken it into many pieces, and has shared the one Cup with everyone, he symbolically multiplies and distributes that unity—completing a most holy Divine Service through these acts. For the unity, simplicity, and hidden nature of Jesus—the Divinely supreme Word—was revealed when he became Man among us.

<meta>This page continues the explanation of the Divine Service (the Eucharist), describing how the liturgical actions of the Hierarch—specifically the unveiling and distribution of the elements—represent Christ’s Incarnation and His invitation for humanity to return to unity with God.</meta>

<header>ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY.</header>
<page-num>70</page-num>

Out of goodness and love for humanity, God descended to our material and visible world <note>original: "the compound and visible"</note> and kindly designed the communion that makes us one with Himself. He united our lowliness to His own divinity <note>original: "Godhead"</note> in the highest degree. This is true if we have been joined to Him as members of a body, through the identity of a blameless and Divine life, and have not become disconnected and separated <note>original: "unsuited and unattached and unyoked"</note> from the healthy, godly members by being spiritually killed by destructive passions. For if we desire communion with Him, we must keep our eyes fixed on His most holy life in the flesh. We must return to a Godlike and blameless state of mind by becoming like Him in His holy sinlessness. In this way, He will share with us a harmonious likeness to Himself.

The Hierarch <term>Hierarch</term> <note>The bishop or chief priest presiding over the liturgy.</note> makes these truths known to those living a religious life by bringing the veiled gifts <note>The bread and wine of the Eucharist, which were covered with a cloth earlier in the service.</note> into view. He divides their unity into many parts, making the people partakers in them through the perfect oneness of the things distributed with those who receive them. Through these visible actions, he depicts our spiritual life in the person of Jesus Christ. He shows Him coming from the hiddenness of the Divine Father and, out of love for humanity, being molded into our likeness through a perfect and unconfused incarnation <term>incarnation</term> in our race. He shows Him entering our divided human condition without ever losing His own essential unity. He calls the human race, through this kind love, into communion with Himself and His blessings—provided we are united to His most Divine life by becoming like it, as far as is permitted. In this way, we truly become participants in God and Divine things. 

After receiving and distributing the Divine Communion, he concludes with the singing of a holy thanksgiving in which the entire congregation <note>original: "the whole body of the Church"</note> takes part. For the Hierarch's own communion comes before the distribution, and his own reception of the mysteries <term>mysteries</term> <note>The sacraments, specifically the Eucharist.</note> comes before he imparts them to others. This is the rule and order of the Divine Mysteries: the reverend leader should first partake and be filled with the gifts from God that are to be shared through him, and only then should he share them with others. 

Therefore, those who recklessly satisfy themselves with Divine teachings while ignoring a life and character that matches those teachings are irreverent <note>original: "profane"</note> and entirely outside the established sacred order. Just as with the bright shining of the sun—where the most delicate and clear objects are filled first with the flowing brilliance and then pass that overflowing light to things near them—it is not acceptable for someone to become a leader in Divine matters if they have not become completely Godlike in character and proven that they are governed by Divine influence and judgment. When the entire order of the priests has gathered together in

It also emphasizes that a leader in the Church must first embody Divine virtues before attempting to lead others.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the explanation of the Eucharist (Synaxis) and begins the fourth chapter, which focuses on the consecration and significance of the holy Myrrh, or Chrism, used in the rituals of the Church.</meta>

<header>ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY.</header>
<page-num>71</page-num>

After those of the priestly order <note>original: "sacerdotal order"</note> have participated in the most divine mysteries, they conclude with a holy thanksgiving. They do this after they have acknowledged and celebrated the gifts of God's works, according to their own spiritual rank. Therefore, those who have not participated in or acknowledged the divine mysteries would not join in the thanksgiving, even though these most divine gifts are, in their essential nature, worthy of thanksgiving. But, as I said, because they have not wished even to look at the divine gifts due to their tendency toward evil, they have remained entirely unblessed regarding the boundless gifts of God’s works. “Taste and see,” say the Scriptures <note>original: "oracles"; a reference to Psalm 34:8</note>, for through sacred instruction in divine matters, the students recognize their generous gifts. By gazing upon the divine height and breadth of these gifts through participation, they praise the heavenly and benevolent works of the Godhead.

---

# CHAPTER IV <note>original: "CAPUT IV"</note>

->*Concerning the rituals of initiation using Myrrh <term>Myrrh</term> <note>In this context, Myrrh refers to the "Holy Chrism," a consecrated oil used for anointing in the sacraments of Confirmation and Ordination.</note>, and those who are perfected by them.*<-

So great and so beautiful are the spiritual visions of the most holy Synaxis <term>Synaxis</term> <note>The formal gathering for the Eucharist or Holy Communion.</note> which, as we have often said, achieve through religious rites our participation in and our movement toward the One. But there is another perfecting service that occupies the same rank as this, which our leaders call the "Initiation of Myrrh." By contemplating its various parts in their proper order, following the rule of sacred symbols <note>original: "images"</note>, we shall be led through religious contemplation from these individual parts back to its essential unity.

## II.—*The Mystery of the Consecration of Myrrh.*

In the same way as in the Synaxis, the ranks of the uninitiated <note>original: "imperfect"</note> are dismissed. This occurs after the Bishop <term>Bishop</term> <note>original: "Hierarch"</note> has made a full circuit of the temple, accompanied by fragrant incense, the chanting of the Psalms, and the reading of the most divine Lections <term>Lections</term> <note>Scripture readings appointed for the service.</note>. Then the Bishop takes the Myrrh and places it upon the Divine Altar, veiled under twelve sacred wings <note>This refers to the "ripidia" or liturgical fans used to cover the sacred elements, often decorated with the faces of six-winged Seraphim.</note>, while everyone cries aloud with a devout voice the sacred melody of the divinely inspired prophets. When the Bishop has finished the prayer offered over the Myrrh, he uses it in the most holy consecrations of things that are hallowed, completing almost every hierarchical function.

## III.—*Contemplation.*

The introductory teaching of this most perfecting ritual, through the rites performed regarding the Divine Myrrh, shows this (in my judgment): that which is holy and of sweet fragrance <note>original: "savour"</note>...

<header>ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY.</header>
<page-num>72</page-num>

...in the minds of devout people, it is covered as if with a veil. This is because holy people are divinely commanded to keep their beautiful and fragrant <note>original: "well-savoured"</note> likeness to the virtue of the hidden God private, rather than making it known for the sake of vanity. For the hidden and sweet beauty of God is pure and is spiritually revealed only to those who contemplate spiritual things and desire to keep the untainted images of virtue within their souls. 

When a person gazes with focused vision upon that spiritual and fragrant beauty, the unpainted but well-imitated image of God-like virtue shapes itself into a most glorious imitation. Just as in physical art, if the artist looks without distraction at the original model <note>original: "archetypal form"</note>—not being drawn away by any other sight or distracted in any way—he will, in a sense, duplicate the very thing he is engraving. He will show the reality within the likeness, and the model within the image, each reflecting the other, except for the difference in their physical substance. In the same way, for those who love mental beauty, the persistent and steady contemplation of this sweet-smelling and hidden beauty will grant them an unerring and most God-like appearance.¹ 

Naturally, then, these "divine copyists"—who steadily mold their own spiritual images after the heavenly fragrance and spiritual beauty—perform none of their divinely-inspired virtues "to be seen by men,"² as the Holy Scripture expresses it. Instead, they reverently contemplate the most holy things of the Church, which are veiled within the holy Myrrh <term>Myrrh</term> <note>The "Holy Myrrh" or Chrism represents the fragrance of the Holy Spirit and the virtues of Christ.</note> as if in a symbolic figure. Therefore, while religiously concealing the holy and most divine virtues within their God-like and God-engraved minds, they look only to the original divine conception. They are not only blind to things that are unlike God, but they do not even stoop to look at them. 

Because of this, as fits their character, they do not love things that merely *seem* to be good and just, but rather those that truly are. They do not look to a mere outward appearance, for which the common crowd unreasonably congratulates itself. Instead, following the Divine example, they distinguish good from evil as they truly are. They are living images of the Supreme Divine Sweetness <note>original: "Suavity"</note>, which, possessing its true fragrance within itself, does not change itself to suit the inconsistent opinions of the multitude, but instead shapes its own genuine nature into true images of itself.

Come, then, since we have viewed the outward beauty of this Divine Service—which is beautiful throughout—let us look toward its even more divine beauty. While the service itself has pulled back the veils surrounding it, let us contemplate its blessed radiance, which shines its bright beams openly and fills us with a fragrance revealed to spiritual people. For the public consecration of the Myrrh is neither hidden from, nor invisible to, those who surround it...
