...the Hierarch—but on the contrary, it is extended to them. But since contemplating this is beyond the reach of most people, they reverently hide it; and by the direction of the Church leadership <note>original: "Hierarchical direction"</note>, it is kept away from the gaze of the multitude.

For the splendor of all holy things sheds its light clearly and without symbols to inspired people, as it is suited to those who are spiritual. It fills their mental understanding with fragrance without any concealment. However, it does not reach those in the lower ranks in the same way. Furthermore, those who contemplate spiritual things deeply hide them under the mystery of the wings <note>The "enigmas of the wings" refers to the liturgical imagery of the Seraphim covering their faces, symbolizing that some divine truths are too intense for direct human sight.</note>, without any show of pride, so that these truths are not defiled by the impure. Through these sacred mysteries <note>original: "enigmas"</note>, the well-ordered lower ranks are guided toward the degree of holiness that matches their own abilities. 

The holy consecration that we are now praising is, as I said, part of the most perfecting order and capacity of the church's functions. For this reason, our Divine Leaders <note>The Apostles or early Church fathers.</note> arranged this ceremony to have the same rank and effect as the holy ceremony of the Synaxis <term>Synaxis</term> <note>The "Synaxis" is the formal gathering for the Eucharist or Mass.</note>. It uses most of the same symbols, mystical rules, and Scripture readings <note>original: "lections"</note>. 

You may also see the Hierarch <term>Hierarch</term> <note>A high-ranking religious leader, such as a Bishop.</note> carrying the sweet perfume from the most holy place into the sacred areas beyond. By returning to the same spot, he teaches that while all holy people participate in Divine things according to their readiness, the Divine itself remains undiminished and entirely unchanged; it stands unmoveable in its own identity with a fixed Divine nature. 

In the same way, the hymns and the readings of the Scriptures bring about a spiritual birth for those not yet fully grown in the life-giving process of being adopted as God’s children. These readings also create a religious mindset in those who are troubled by evil <note>original: "accursed"</note> spirits. They drive away fear and spiritual weakness <note>original: "unmanliness"</note> from those who lack courage, showing them—according to what they can handle—the highest peak of God-like character and power. By following this example, they will instead scare away opposing forces and take the lead in healing others. By following God’s example and remaining firm in their own spiritual gifts, they will not only act against those fears but will also inspire activity in others. 

These rituals also give a holy persistence to those who have turned from evil to a religious mind, so that they will not be enslaved by evil again. They completely purify those who need to become entirely pure. They lead the holy toward their own Divine likeness, contemplation, and communion. They also establish those who are already holy in blessed and spiritual visions, perfecting their likeness to the One God and making them one with Him. 

What more can I say? Is it not these same ranks we have mentioned—those who are not yet entirely pure—whom this religious service excludes just like the Synaxis? It is intended to be seen by the holy alone, through symbols, and is con—

...is contemplated and performed by only those who are perfectly holy, without symbols, through instructions given by the Hierarchy? I believe it is unnecessary to repeat this, as I have mentioned it many times already. 

Those who see with spiritual insight can rightly contemplate the Hierarch holding the Divine Myrrh <term>Myrrh</term> <note>In this context, the "Myrrh" refers to the sacred Chrism oil used for anointing, symbolizing the Holy Spirit and the fragrance of Christ.</note>, which is veiled under twelve wings, as he devoutly completes the holy service of consecration. We can say that the composition of the Myrrh is a blend of aromatic materials with naturally fragrant qualities. Those who share in it are perfumed by it, each according to how much they receive of its sweet scent. 

We are convinced that the most Divine Jesus is beyond all essence <note>original: "superessentially"</note> a source of goodness, filling our souls with a divine sweetness through his spiritual gifts. Just as physical scents bring joy and nourishment to our sense of smell—assuming our senses are healthy and attuned to the fragrance—so our spiritual faculties can receive a "sweet scent" from God. If our minds are healthy and free from evil, using the natural ability to discern that God gave us, we are filled with holy comfort and divine nourishment. This happens according to God's plan and how well our minds are focused on the Divine. 

Therefore, the symbolic composition of the Myrrh gives form to things that have no physical form. It portrays Jesus himself as a well-spring of divine sweetness <note>original: Cant. i. 3; "Your name is oil poured out."</note>. He distributes these divine perfumes in specific degrees to those spiritual people who are most like God. Their minds are filled with comforting joy and holy inspiration, enjoying spiritual nourishment as these fragrant gifts <note>original: 2 Cor. ii. 14; "The fragrance of His knowledge."</note> enter their spiritual nature through divine participation. 

I believe it is clear that the distribution of this primary fragrance to the heavenly beings above us is more direct because they are more divine. It reveals itself more fully to their radiant transparency, overflowing into their healthy minds without restraint. However, for lower minds that are less receptive, the highest level of vision and participation is kept hidden. The fragrance is distributed to them in a measured proportion that matches their ability to receive it. 

Among these holy beings above us, the highest rank—the Seraphim <term>Seraphim</term> <note>The highest order of angels, often depicted in this liturgy by twelve wings (representing two six-winged Seraphim) covering the holy mysteries.</note>—is represented by the figure of the twelve wings. They are positioned around Jesus, devoting themselves to the most blessed contemplation allowed to them, and are filled with spiritual gifts through their holy reception of them, and, to...

For the sacred knowledge of these celestial minds is untiring; they possess Divine love without interruption, and they are superior to all baseness and forgetfulness. Therefore, I believe the phrase "unceasing cry" suggests their constant and persistent knowledge and understanding of Divine things, joined with complete resolve and thanksgiving. I believe we have now sufficiently reflected on the distinctive features of the bodiless <term>Seraphim</term> <note>original: "Seraphin." From the Hebrew for "burning ones," these are the highest rank of angels.</note> as they are described in the Heavenly Hierarchy. These beings are portrayed in the Scriptures through physical symbols to explain spiritual realities, and we have made them clear to your inner vision. Nevertheless, since those who stand reverently around the <term>Hierarch</term> <note>The "Hierarch" is the high-ranking clergyman, such as a bishop, who leads the sacred rites.</note> reflect that same order—the highest of all in knowledge—we will now observe their most God-like glory through spiritual vision.

Their countless faces and many feet show, I believe, their ability to view Divine illumination from many sides, and their understanding of God's goodness as something ever-active and received in abundance. The six-fold arrangement of their wings, mentioned in the Scriptures, does not signify a sacred number. Instead, I believe it shows that the first, middle, and last spiritual and God-like powers of the highest Order around God are elevating, entirely free, and otherworldly <note>original: "super-mundane"</note>. Thus, the holy wisdom of the Scriptures describes the arrangement of the wings around their heads, <note>Isaiah 6:2</note> their midsections, and their feet. This suggests they are completely covered by wings and possess the ability to lead others toward the Truly Existing <note>The "Truly Existing" refers to God as the source of all being.</note>.

If they cover their heads and their feet and fly only with their middle wings, keep this sacred truth in mind: this Order, which is so far above the highest beings, is careful regarding the most lofty and deep of its insights. It raises itself in proper proportion to the vision of God by its middle wings, placing its life under Divine guidance <note>original: "yokes"</note>, which directs it toward the knowledge of itself. Regarding the statement in Holy Scripture that "one cried out to the other," I believe this shows that they share their own visions of God with one another without hesitation. We should also find it worthy of sacred memory that the Hebrew word used in the Holy Scriptures for the most holy <term>Seraphim</term> is a descriptive name meaning "burning" or "seething," signifying their Divine and constantly moving life.

The divine <term>Seraphim</term> <note>original: "Seraphin." From the Hebrew for "burning ones."</note> are named by the Word of God as "glowing" and "heating," names that express their essential nature. According to the symbolic description of the holy <term>Myrrh</term>, <note>original: "Divine Myrrh." In this context, it refers to "Chrism," the consecrated oil used in church rituals.</note> they possess ever-moving powers that reveal the distribution of its uplifting fragrance. For the incomprehensible and kind Being loves to be moved into manifestation by glowing and pure minds; He imparts his most divine inspirations in joyful portions to those who devoutly call Him forth. 

In this way, the most exalted order of heavenly beings did not fail to recognize <note>1 Timothy 3:16: "And without controversy great is the mystery of godliness: God was manifest in the flesh..."</note> the supremely divine Jesus when He descended for the purpose of being sanctified. Instead, they observed Him humbling Himself to take on our nature through divine and inexpressible goodness. When they saw Him sanctified in a human way—by the Father, <note>John 10:36: "Say ye of him, whom the Father hath sanctified, and sent into the world, Thou blasphemest; because I said, I am the Son of God?"</note> Himself, <note>John 17:19: "And for their sakes I sanctify myself..."</note> and the Holy Spirit <note>Romans 1:4: "And declared to be the Son of God with power, according to the spirit of holiness..."</note>—they recognized their own supreme Head as remaining essentially unchanged in His divinity, regardless of His actions. 

Therefore, the teaching of the sacred symbols places the Seraphim near the holy Myrrh when it is being consecrated. This recognizes and describes Christ as unchanged in His complete incarnation among us in the true nature of humanity. What is even more divine is that the Church uses this holy Myrrh for the perfection of every religious function. This clearly shows, according to the divine word, that the sanctified Sanctifier remains always the same in Himself throughout the entire course of His divine goodness. 

For this reason, the finishing gift and grace of divine <term>regeneration</term> <note>The process of being "born again" through baptism.</note> is completed in the divine perfection of the Myrrh. Therefore, I believe that when the <term>Hierarch</term> <note>The high-ranking clergyman or bishop.</note> pours the Myrrh into the purifying baptismal font in the shape of a cross, <note>Philippians 2:8: "And being found in fashion as a man, he humbled himself, and became obedient unto death, even the death of the cross."</note> he reveals to our spiritual eyes how Jesus descended into death itself through the cross to bring about our birth in God. By that same divine and irresistible descent, He draws up from the jaws of a destructive death those who, according to the mysterious saying, "are baptized into His death." <note>Romans 6:3: "Know ye not, that so many of us as were baptized into Jesus Christ were baptized into his death?"</note> He rescues them from the former abyss of a destructive <note>Romans 8:2: "For the law of the Spirit of life in Christ Jesus hath made me free from the law of sin and death."</note> death and renews them to a godly and eternal existence. <note>Romans 6:4: "Therefore we are buried with him by baptism into death..."</note>

Furthermore, the perfecting <term>unction</term> <note>The act of anointing with oil.</note> of the Myrrh grants the indwelling of the supremely divine Spirit to the person who has been initiated into the most sacred birth in God. I believe the sacred imagery of these symbols suggests the gift of the Divine Spirit, given by Him who was sanctified as a man for our sakes by the Divine Spirit, while remaining unchanged in His essential Godhead. 

Bear this also most religiously in mind: the law of the most holy completion requires the sacred <term>consecration</term> <note>The act of making something holy or dedicated to a divine purpose.</note> of the Divine Altar to be finished by pure pourings of the most holy Myrrh. But this work of God, which is at once beyond the heavens and—

One might call it the "Completion of God"—celebrating its divine completing work in two ways: for his completion is both the sanctification of himself as a man on our behalf, and the completing and sanctifying of all things by his power as God. As for the sacred melody of the inspired and God-filled prophets, it is called by those who know Hebrew, "Praise of God," or "Praise the Lord" <note>This is a translation of the Hebrew "Hallelujah."</note>. Since every divine manifestation and work of God is religiously portrayed in the various symbols of the hierarchy, it is appropriate to mention the divinely inspired song of the prophets; it teaches clearly and reverently that the generous works of divine goodness are worthy of devout praise.

### CHAPTER 5 <note>original: "CAPUT V."</note>

->*Concerning priestly consecrations.*<- <note>original: "sacerdotal consecrations."</note>

Such, then, is the most divine and perfecting work of the Myrrh. This will demonstrate that the harmonious order of our hierarchy entirely rejects and excludes what is disordered, unregulated, or confused; at the same time, it reveals the regulated, ordered, and well-established structure within the ranks of the sacred orders. Now, as I believe we have shown in the hierarchy already discussed, every hierarchy has a threefold division—since our sacred tradition holds that every hierarchical function is divided...

Therefore, the most Divine Hierarchy of the heavenly Beings has for its initiation its own unique and most immaterial understanding of God and Divine matters, according to its capacity. It possesses a complete likeness to God and a constant practice of imitating God, as far as is permitted. The ones who illuminate and lead others to this sacred consecration are the very first Beings surrounding God. These Beings generously and in proper measure transmit the knowledge given to them to the sacred ranks beneath them. This knowledge is always deifying, coming from the self-existing and supreme Deity who rules the Divine minds that impart wisdom to humanity. The ranks that are subordinate to these first Beings are truly called the "initiated orders," because they are led in a religious manner through those higher beings toward the deifying illumination of the supreme Deity. 

After this heavenly and super-worldly <term>Hierarchy</term> <note>original: "supermundane Hierarchy." Referring to the angelic orders that exist beyond the physical world.</note>, the supreme Deity established the Hierarchy under the Law <note>The "Hierarchy under the Law" refers to the religious system of the Old Testament established through Moses.</note>. This was for the purpose of sharing its most holy gifts for the benefit of our race, treating us, as it were, like children¹; it gave a measured light so as not to overwhelm weak vision. It provided copies² that were far removed from the <term>Archetypes</term> <note>original: "Archetypes." The original, perfect heavenly patterns upon which earthly symbols are based.</note> and were difficult to understand³, as well as symbols whose hidden meaning was not easily seen⁴. 

Now, the initiation of the Hierarchy under the Law is the restoration to spiritual worship⁵. Our leaders in this were men religiously instructed in the holy tabernacle⁶ by Moses, the first initiator and leader of the Hierarchs under the Law. In reference to this tabernacle, when describing the Hierarchy under the Law for the purpose of instruction, Moses called all the sacred services of the Law an image of the pattern shown to him on Mount Sinai⁷. However, the "initiated" are those who are being led from the symbols of the Law toward a more perfect revelation, in proportion to their capacity. 

The Word of God calls our own Hierarchy "the more perfect revelation," naming it a fulfillment⁸ of the Law and a holy inheritance. Our Hierarchy is both celestial and legal, acting as a middle ground between the two extremes. It shares with the heavenly hierarchy through spiritual contemplation, but it also shares with the Law because it is expressed through <term>sensible signs</term> <note>original: "sensible signs." Physical objects or rituals that can be perceived by the five senses, such as water, oil, bread, and wine.</note>, and through these, we are reverently led toward the Divine. There is likewise a threefold division of our Hierarchy: it is divided into the most holy services of the initiations, the God-like ministers of holy things, and those who are being led by them, according to their capacity, to the holy rites.

But each of the threefold divisions of our Hierarchy, like that—

<header>ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY.</header>

The hierarchy of the Law <note>The religious system of the Old Testament.</note> and the Hierarchy that is more Divine than ours <note>The celestial or angelic hierarchy.</note> are both arranged into first, middle, and last ranks. Each carefully maintains the proper balance of religious rites and the well-ordered fellowship that keeps everything in harmony and binds all things together. 

The most holy service of the initiations <note>The sacraments or sacred rites of the Church.</note> treats the sacred cleansing of the imperfect as the first God-like rank. The middle rank is the illuminating instruction of those who have already been purified. The last rank—which is the completion of the first two—is the perfecting of those who have been instructed in an exact knowledge of the revelations they have received. 

The Order of Ministers also follows this structure. In the first rank, the ministers cleanse the uninitiated through the introductory rites. In the middle rank, they impart light to those who have been cleansed. In the final and highest rank of ministry, they complete the training of those who have been made partakers of the light, using their expert skill in the divine illuminations being studied. 

From the perspective of those being initiated, the stages are as follows: 
1. The first rank consists of those being purified. 
2. The next rank consists of those being illuminated after their purification; these individuals contemplate sacred mysteries <note>original: "mysteries." These are the deeper spiritual truths revealed to the faithful.</note>. 
3. The final rank, which is more Divine than the others, consists of those being enlightened with the most perfecting knowledge of the sacred truths they observe. 

This threefold rank of holy service is celebrated in the following way: "Birth in God" [Baptism] is described in the sacred oracles <note>The Holy Scriptures.</note> as both a purification and a light-giving illumination. Meanwhile, the initiation of the **Synaxis** <note>original: "Synaxis." The gathering for the Eucharist or Holy Communion.</note> and the **Myrrh** <note>original: "Myrrh." The holy anointing oil, or Chrism, used in consecration.</note> serve as the perfecting knowledge and understanding of God's works. Through these, the soul's unifying rise toward the Godhead and its most blessed communion with Him is reverently perfected. 

Now, let us next examine the priestly <note>original: "sacerdotal."</note> order, which is divided into the disciplines of purifying, illuminating, and perfecting. It is the sacred Law of the Deity that the lower ranks are led toward His most Divine Light through the higher ranks. Do we not see this even in the physical world? Material elements first approach things that are most like themselves, and through those substances, they spread their energy to other things. 

Naturally, then, the Source and Foundation of all order—both invisible and visible—causes the Divinely-wrought rays of light to first reach the more Divine Beings. Because these beings are more transparent minds, better suited to receive and transmit Light, God transmits His light and His manifestations through them to the lower ranks in proportions suitable for them.

Therefore, it is the duty of these "first contemplators" <note>The highest rank of the hierarchy who "see" the divine truths first.</note> to generously show the Divine visions they have reverently gazed upon to the next order, according to that order's capacity. They reveal the matters relating to the Hierarchy because they themselves have been fully instructed in the complete knowledge of all these things...

¹ Plato, *Phaedo* 1. 52. <note>original: "Phæd."</note> 
² Plato, *Phaedo* 1. 54.

It emphasizes that divine light is transmitted from God through higher beings to lower ones according to their capacity.</summary>

<header>ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY.</header>

...regarding their own hierarchy, and have received the effective power of instruction. They impart sacred gifts according to each person's ability to receive them, since they possess hierarchical completeness in the highest degree of knowledge and achievement. 

The divine order of the Hierarchs <note>original: "Hierarchs"; usually referring to Bishops, who hold the highest office in the church hierarchy.</note> is the first of the contemplative orders. However, it is simultaneously the highest and the lowest, because every order within our hierarchy is summed up and fulfilled within it. Just as we see the entire hierarchy ending in Jesus, we see each specific order summed up in its own godly Hierarch. 

The power of the Hierarchical order spreads through the entire sacred body and, through every one of the sacred orders, it performs the mysteries of its specific hierarchy. However, divine law has assigned the most sacred services of the sanctuary to the Hierarch as his special work, rather than to the other orders. These services are the perfecting images of supreme divine power, completing all the most divine symbols and all the sacred ranks. 

For although some of the venerable symbols are consecrated by the priests, a priest will never bring about the "Divine Birth" <note>Baptism.</note> without the most sacred Myrrh <note>Holy oil used for anointing; also called Chrism.</note>; nor will he consecrate the mysteries of the Divine Communion unless the symbols of Communion have been placed upon the most divine altar. Furthermore, he would not be a priest at all unless he had been appointed to this role through Hierarchical consecration. 

Therefore, divine law has uniquely assigned the following tasks to the perfecting powers of the inspired Hierarchs: the dedication of the hierarchical orders, the consecration of the sacred Myrrh, and the full consecration of the altar. It is the Hierarchical order, then, which is equipped with the power to perfect. It primarily completes the perfecting functions of the hierarchy, clearly reveals the science of the holy mysteries, and teaches their proper balance, sacred conditions, and powers. 

The enlightened order of the priests, however, leads those being initiated toward the divine visions of the sacred rites under the direction of the inspired Hierarch. In cooperation with the Hierarch, the priest reverently performs his own religious duties. Whatever this order does—whether showing the works of God through holy symbols, perfecting those who approach through divine contemplation, or participating in the hallowed initiations—it still refers those who seek an exact knowledge of the religious service they have witnessed to the Hierarch. 

The order of the <term>Ministers</term> <note>original: "Leitourgoi"; the Greek term for ministers or deacons.</note>, which is the rank that purifies, has the function of separating those who are unfit before the priests begin the divine service. They purify those who are approaching, making them entirely clean from contrary behaviors and suitable for the sanctifying vision and communion. For this reason, during the service of Regeneration <note>Baptism.</note>, the <term>Ministers</term> strip the person approaching of their old clothing and even take off their sandals, making them stand...

<meta>This page continues the explanation of the three-fold ecclesiastical hierarchy (Ministers, Priests, and Hierarchs), detailing their specific duties in purification, illumination, and perfection. <header>ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY.</header>

...they lead him towards the west for renunciation [of his former life]. And again, they lead him back to the east—for they belong to the purifying order and function—commanding those who approach to entirely cast away the "coverings" of their former life. By showing the darkness of their former conduct, they teach those who have said farewell to that darkness how to transfer their allegiance to the Light. The Order of the <term>Ministers</term> <note>original: "Leitourgoi"; the deacons or assistants who serve the liturgy.</note>, then, is for purification. They lead those who have been purified to the bright services of the Priests. They both purify and bring the uninitiated to "Spiritual Birth" <note>Baptism.</note> through the purifying light and teachings of the sacred scriptures. Furthermore, they turn away the unholy from the Priests without favoritism. For this reason, the Hierarchical institution places them over the holy gates, suggesting that those who come to holy things must do so with complete purification. They guide the approach to a reverent vision and communion through these purifying functions, admitting people through these gates only when they are without blemish.

We have shown, then, that the order of the <term>Hierarchs</term> <note>original: "Hierarchs"; refers to the bishops, who hold the highest office.</note> is for perfecting and completing—that of the Priests is for illuminating and conducting to the light—and that of the <term>Ministers</term> is for purifying and discerning. That is to say, the Hierarchical order is appointed not only to perfect but also, at the same time, to enlighten and to purify. The rank of the Priests possesses within itself the science of purification along with the power of illumination. An inferior rank cannot step up to perform superior functions; in addition to this, it is not permitted for them to undertake such unauthorized practices. However, the more divine, higher orders possess the sacred science of the subordinate orders along with their own. 

Nevertheless, since the priestly orders are images of Divine Energies, they are arranged in hierarchical distinctions. They show within themselves the regulated flow of light into the first, middle, and last sacred energies and orders of God's well-regulated and distinct activities. As I have said, they manifest in themselves the orderly and distinct character of the Divine Energies. For since the supreme Deity first cleanses the minds He enters, then enlightens them, and once enlightened, perfects them to a God-like perfection, the Hierarchical image of the Divine naturally divides itself into well-defined orders and functions. This clearly shows the Divine Energies firmly established without confusion in the most hallowed and distinct ranks. Now, since we have spoken—as far as we are permitted—of the priestly orders and their completeness, their powers and their activities, let us now contemplate their most holy consecration ceremonies as well as we can.

### II.—*Mystery of priestly consecrations.*

<header>ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY.</header>

...the God-transmitted oracles <note>original: "oracles"; here referring to the Gospels or Holy Scriptures.</note>, and the Hierarch’s right hand; in this manner, he is consecrated <note>original: "ἀποτελειουται" (apoteleiotai); to be perfected or fully initiated.</note> by the Hierarch who ordains him with all the holy prayers. The Priest, after he has bent both his knees before the Divine Altar, has the Hierarch’s right hand placed upon his head; in this manner, he is dedicated <note>original: "ἁγιάσεται" (hagiasetai); to be made holy or hallowed.</note> by the ordaining Hierarch with hallowing prayers. But the <term>Minister</term> <note>original: "Leitourgos"; a deacon or assistant who serves the liturgy.</note>, after he has bent only one of his two knees before the Divine Altar, has the right hand of the ordaining Hierarch upon his head, being ordained <note>original: "τελειούμενος" (teleioumenos); being perfected or initiated.</note> by him with the consecrating prayers used in the ordination of Ministers. Upon each of them, the seal of the cross <note>original: "σφραγὶς" (sphragis); a seal or signet.</note> is impressed by the ordaining Hierarch. At each ordination, the sacred proclamation of the person's name takes place, followed by the final salutation; for every member of the priesthood present, as well as the Hierarch who performed the ordination, greets the person who has been consecrated to any of these three hierarchical orders.

### III.—*Contemplation* <note>In the writings of Dionysius, "Contemplation" refers to the spiritual or symbolic interpretation of the outward rituals.</note>

These things, then, are common to the <term>Hierarch</term> <note>original: "Hierarch"; the highest order, such as a Bishop.</note>, the Priests, and the Ministers: being led to the Divine Altar, the act of kneeling, the laying on of the Hierarch’s right hand, the sign of the cross, the announcement of the name, and the concluding greeting.

However, the special and unique rite for Hierarchs is the placing of the Holy Scriptures upon the head, a rite which the subordinate orders do not receive. For Priests, the distinction is the bending of both knees, which the consecration of Ministers does not include; for Ministers, as has been said, bend only *one* of their two knees. 

The act of being led to the Altar and kneeling suggests to those being ordained that their lives are placed entirely under God, the source of all consecration. It shows that their whole intellectual self approaches Him in total purity and holiness, striving to be as like Him as possible, and becoming fit for the Divine and most holy Victim <note>original: "Victim"; referring to Christ.</note> and the Altar, which purifies the God-seeking mind through these sacred services. 

The laying on of the Hierarch’s right hand signifies the protection of God, the primary Consecrator. Under Him, like holy children, the clergy are cherished with a father's care. At the same time, this gesture grants them their priestly character and role, and drives away any opposing [spiritual] forces. It also teaches that all priestly actions are effective only insofar as those who have been consecrated act under God’s authority and have Him as the Leader in every aspect of their lives. Finally, the marking with the sign of the Cross represents the silencing of all fleshly impulses and the beginning of a life of Divine imitation—a life that looks unfailingly to the life of Jesus, who is both...

<meta>This page continues the "Contemplation" section, specifically interpreting the meaning behind the announcement of the candidate's name and the role of Divine choice in ordination, drawing parallels with Moses and the Apostles.</meta>

<header>ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY.</header>

...courageous <note>original: "manly" (Greek: ἀνδρικῶς); here implying strength and virtue.</note> and most godly, who has come even to the Cross and death with a supremely Divine sinlessness, and marks those who live in this way with his own likeness through the cruciform sign <note>The sign of the cross.</note> of his own sinlessness.

But the <term>Hierarch</term> <note>The bishop or chief presiding officer of the sacred rites.</note> calls aloud the name of the consecrations and of the persons being consecrated. The mysterious meaning of this is that the holy consecrator, who is beloved of God, reveals God's own supreme choice. He does not lead those who are being ordained to their priestly <note>original: "sacerdotal"</note> consecration of his own accord or by his own favor, but acts as one moved by God in all these hierarchical dedications. Thus Moses, the consecrator under the Law, did not lead his brother Aaron to priestly consecration—even though he considered him both beloved of God and holy—until he was moved to do so by God. In submission to God as the Chief Consecrator, Moses then completed the priestly consecration through hierarchical rites.1 Furthermore, our supremely Divine and first Consecrator (for Jesus, out of His great love for humanity, became even this), did "not glorify Himself," as the Scriptures say, but was glorified by the One who said to Him, "You2 are a Priest forever after the order of Melchizedek." 

Therefore, when Jesus led the disciples to priestly consecration, although He is the Chief Consecrator as God, He nevertheless refers the hierarchical completion of the work of consecration to His most holy Father and the supremely Divine Spirit. He did this by instructing the disciples not to depart from Jerusalem, but to "wait for the promise of the Father, which you heard from me: that you shall be baptized in the Holy Spirit."3 And indeed, the leader <note>original: "coryphæus" (Greek: κορυφαῖος); referring to Saint Peter as the head of the apostles.</note> of the disciples himself, along with the ten others of the same rank and hierarchy as himself, when he proceeded to complete the number of the disciples to twelve, reverently left the selection to the Supreme Deity. He said, "Show4 whom You have chosen," and received the man who was divinely designated by the Divine choice into the number of the twelve Hierarchs. 

Now, concerning the "Divine lot" which fell as a sign from God upon Matthias, others have expressed different views which I believe are incorrect; I will share my own perspective. It seems to me that the Scriptures use the word "lot" to describe a certain gift from God, pointing out to that Hierarchical group the person who had been designated by Divine election. This is especially significant because the Divine Hierarch must not perform a priestly consecration by his own impulse, but as a servant of God who moves him to accomplish these consecrations with religious rites and heavenly signs.

Now, the salutation <note>The ritual kiss of peace or greeting.</note> at the end of the consecration has a religious significance. For all the members of the priestly orders who are present, as well as the Hierarch who has consecrated them, salute...

He is elevated to a most God-like beauty <note>original: "comeliness"</note>; he loves those with minds similar to his own and is religiously loved by them in return. This is why the mutual priestly greeting <note>The kiss of peace or fraternal embrace among the clergy.</note> is performed, proclaiming the religious communion of minds of like character and their loving kindness toward each other. This greeting maintains their most God-like beauty in complete perfection through priestly training.

These things, as I said, are common to the entire priestly consecration. The <term>Hierarch</term> <note>The bishop or chief presiding officer.</note>, however, has a unique distinction: the scriptures <note>original: "oracles" (Greek: logia); here referring specifically to the Book of the Gospels.</note> are placed most reverently upon his head. Since the perfecting function and knowledge of the whole Priesthood is conferred upon the inspired Hierarchs by the supreme Divine and perfecting goodness, it is natural that the Divinely transmitted scriptures are placed upon the heads of the Hierarchs. These scriptures explain completely and systematically all theology: the work of God, the manifestation of God, the sacred word, and the sacred work. In one word, they contain all the Divine and sacred works and words bequeathed to our <term>Hierarchy</term> <note>The organized body of the church and its sacred orders.</note> by the benevolent Deity. 

Because the God-like Hierarch has partaken entirely of the whole Hierarchical power, he will not only be illuminated in the true and God-transmitted knowledge of all the holy words and works committed to the Hierarchy, but he will also pass them on to others through Hierarchical teaching. Through his power as Hierarch, he will perfect all the most essential functions of the entire Priesthood in the most Divine knowledge and highest instruction. 

The distinctive feature of the ordination of Priests, as contrasted with the ordination of the <term>Leitourgoi</term> <note>original: "Leitourgoi" (Greek: λειτουργοί); refers here to the lower ministers or deacons.</note>, is the bending of both knees, whereas that lower order bends only one and is consecrated by this religious symbol. This kneeling represents the submissive approach of the person who draws near, who places under God's authority that which is religiously presented. 

But since, as we have often said, the three orders of the consecrators—through the three most holy initiations and functions—preside over the three orders of the initiated, they accomplish the saving approach of these initiates under the Divine yoke. Naturally, the order of Deacons, being focused only on purification, religiously performs the *one* approach (kneeling on one knee) for those who are being purified, placing it before the Divine Altar, as though the minds being purified were being transcendently hallowed in the Deacon himself. 

However, the Priests bend both their knees because those who are religiously brought forward by them are not only being purified, but have been religiously perfected by their enlightened instructions into a state of contemplation and power. But the Hierarch, bending both his knees, has the God-transmitted scriptures upon his head, as he leads—through his office of Hierarch—those who have been purified by the...

## CHAPTER VI. <note>original: "CAPUT VI."</note>

### *Concerning the Ranks of the Initiated.*

These, then, are the priestly <note>original: "sacerdotal"</note> ranks and vocations, their powers, functions, and consecrations. We must next explain the three <note>original: "Triad"</note> ranks of the initiated who are subordinate to them. 

We affirm that the many people who are dismissed from the Divine Services and Consecrations mentioned previously are actually orders undergoing a course of purification. One group is being molded and fashioned for a living existence by the Deacons through the scriptures <note>original: "oracles"</note> that bring them to spiritual birth <note>This refers to the "Catechumens," or those preparing for baptism.</note>. Another group is already being called back to the holy life from which they had departed, guided by the wholesome discipline of the good scriptures <note>This refers to the "Penitents," those who have sinned and are seeking reconciliation.</note>. Another group is being fortified by strengthening scriptures after having been terrified by opposing fears and a lack of spiritual courage <note>Likely referring to the "Energumens," or those troubled by spirits or intense mental/spiritual distress.</note>. Another group is being led back from evil intentions to holy efforts; and yet another has been led back but does not yet possess a pure, fixed stability in more God-like habits and peace. 

These are the orders currently under purification by the nurturing and purifying powers of the ministers <note>original: "Leitourgoi"</note>. Through their sacred powers, the ministers perfect these people so that, after their complete cleansing, they may be brought to contemplate and participate in the most luminous Divine Services.

The middle rank is the contemplative order. This group participates in certain Divine Offices in all purity, according to its capacity, and is assigned to the Priests for its enlightenment. In my opinion, it is evident that this order—having been cleansed from all unholy impurity and having acquired a steadfast mind in perfect purity—is led back through priestly functions to the habit and power of contemplation. They communicate in the most Divine symbols <note>The Eucharist or Holy Communion.</note> and are filled with holy joy in their contemplations and communions. They mount toward the Divine love of their highest knowledge through these instructions and functions, as far as their capacity allows. This, I say, is the order of the holy people <note>The baptized faithful.</note> who, having passed through complete purification, are deemed worthy of both the reverent vision and participation in the most luminous Sacraments.

Finally, the order higher than all the initiated is the sacred order of the Monks. By using all its power, this rank has attained an entirely purified purification and completely...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "initiated" ranks within the church, moving from the groups being purified (catechumens and penitents) to the highest rank of the initiated: the monks.</meta>

<page-num>86</page-num>

<header>ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY.</header>

Having purified its energies, this rank has acquired a spiritual contemplation and participation in every Divine Service which it is permitted to witness. It is guided by the most perfecting functions of the Hierarchs <note>The "Hierarchs" usually refers to the Bishops, who hold the highest office in the church hierarchy.</note> and taught by their inspired illuminations and traditions regarding the Divine Services of the holy Sacraments. Viewed according to their capacity and elevated by sacred science, they are led to the highest level of perfection of which they are capable.

For this reason, our Divine leaders have deemed them worthy of sacred names. Some call them <term>Therapeutæ</term> <note>original: "Therapeutæ," from a Greek word meaning "servants" or "worshippers."</note>, while others call them <term>Monks</term> <note>original: "Monks," from the Greek "monachos," meaning solitary or single.</note> because of their pure service and fervent devotion to the true God. This name also comes from their undivided and unified life, which makes them "one" by folding together their scattered interests into a God-like unity and a God-loving perfection. Therefore, the Divine institution granted them a perfecting grace and deemed them worthy of a specific prayer of consecration. This consecration is not "hierarchical"—as that is reserved for the priestly orders alone—but is "hallowing,"¹ meaning it is a sacred rite performed by the pious Priests in the second degree of hierarchical consecration.

### II.—**MYSTERION.** <note>original: "MYSTERION," the Greek word for "Mystery" or "Sacrament."</note>

### *The Mystery of Monastic Consecration.*

The Priest stands before the Divine Altar and religiously pronounces the invocation for Monks. The candidate for the monkhood stands behind the Priest; he does not bend both knees, nor even one, nor does he have the Divinely transmitted oracles <note>The "oracles" here refer to the Gospels or Holy Scriptures.</note> placed upon his head. Instead, he simply stands near the Priest while the Priest pronounces the mystical Blessing over him. 

When the Priest has finished this, he approaches the man being consecrated and asks him first if he bids farewell to all distractions—not only the distractions of secular life, but even those of the imagination. Then, the Priest sets before him the most perfect way of life and warns him that it is his bounden duty to surpass the standard of an ordinary life. When the initiate has steadfastly promised all these things, and the Priest has marked him with the sign of the Cross, the Priest crops his hair. After an invocation to the <term>threefold subsistence</term> <note>The Trinity: Father, Son, and Holy Spirit.</note> of the Divine Blessedness, the Priest strips off the man's old clothing and clothes him anew. Then, along with all the holy men present, the Priest greets him with a kiss and administers to him the supremely Divine Mysteries <note>The Holy Eucharist or Communion.</note>.

### III.—*Contemplation.*

The fact that the candidate bends neither knee, nor has the Divinely transmitted scriptures placed upon his head, but stands by the Priest while...

<page-num>87</page-num>

<header>ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY.</header>

The priest pronounces the blessing for monks to show that the monastic order is not meant for leading others. Instead, it stands on its own in a secluded and holy state, following the <term>sacerdotal</term> <note>original: "sacerdotal," meaning priestly.</note> orders. Monks are guided by these priests as followers toward the divine science of sacred things, according to their ability to understand. 

By renouncing a life divided by worldly interests—including even their thoughts and imaginations—monks demonstrate the most perfect love of wisdom. This wisdom is practiced through an exact knowledge of the commandments that bring a person into unity with God. As I mentioned before, the monastic rank is not part of the middle order of the initiated; it is the highest rank of all. 

Because of this, many things allowed for those in the middle rank are strictly forbidden to monks. They are obligated to be unified with "the One" <note>This refers to the Neoplatonic and Christian concept of God as the ultimate Unity.</note> and to remain in holy isolation. They must transform their lives to resemble the priestly life as much as possible, since they share a close affinity with it and are closer to it than any other rank of the initiated. 

The marking with the sign of the Cross, as we have already said, represents the silencing of almost all physical desires. The cutting of the hair represents a pure and humble life. Such a life does not try to hide the soul's inner state by "decorating" it with false pretenses. Instead, it leads the soul toward the highest likeness of God through simple, monastic discipline rather than human attractions. 

Changing clothes signifies the transition from an ordinary religious life to a more perfect one. This is similar to the holy "Birth from God" <note>A reference to Baptism.</note>, where changing clothes represented the rise of a purified life to a state of contemplation and enlightenment. When the priest and all the religious people present greet the newly consecrated monk, remember the holy fellowship of those who carry God’s likeness; they congratulate each other with divine joy. 

Finally, as the completion of the rite, the priest invites the newly consecrated monk to the supremely Divine Communion. This ceremony shows that if the monk truly wishes to reach this unified elevation, he must not simply observe the sacred mysteries or receive the symbols in the same way as the middle rank. Instead, because he has received a divine knowledge of the mysteries, he will approach the reception of the Eucharist in a way that is different from the rest of the holy people. 

This is why the Holy <term>Eucharist</term> <note>The sacrament of the Lord's Supper.</note> is also given to the priestly orders during their own dedication to the priesthood. At the conclusion of their sacred consecrations, the <term>Hierarch</term> <note>The Bishop.</note> who has consecrated them administers it. This is not only because receiving the supremely—

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the hierarchy of the Church, moving from the specific initiation of monks to the broader structure of the three sacred orders: purification, illumination, and perfection. It draws a direct parallel between the earthly church hierarchy and the celestial hierarchy of angels.</meta>

<page-num>88</page-num>

<header>ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY.</header>

The Divine Mysteries represent the fulfillment of every reception into the Hierarchy. Because all the sacred orders partake of this common and most godly gift according to their capacity, they are elevated and perfected in their union with God <note>original: "deification."</note>. We conclude, then, that the holy initiations consist of three stages: purification, illumination, and consecration. 

The <term>ministers</term> <note>original: "Leitourgoi," from the Greek for liturgical servants, referring here to the order of deacons.</note> are a purifying order, the priests are an illuminating order, and the godlike <term>hierarchs</term> <note>original: "Hierarchs," meaning bishops or high priests.</note> are a perfecting order. The group excluded from sacred contemplation and communion—because they are still undergoing purification <note>This refers to catechumens (those learning the faith) and penitents.</note>—is the "order being purified." However, the order of the unified monks is a "purified order." Thus, our Hierarchy, reverently arranged in ranks fixed by God, is like the Heavenly Hierarchies; it preserves its godlike characteristics and its imitation of the Divine as far as humans are able.

But you might argue that the orders undergoing purification fall far short of the Heavenly Hierarchies, since it is neither permissible nor true to say that any celestial order is "impure." I would completely agree that the angels are entirely without blemish and possess a purity far above this world; if I thought otherwise, I would have completely lost my religious mind. If any of them had been taken captive by evil and fallen from the heavenly, undefiled harmony of the celestial minds, they would have been cast down to the dark fall of the rebellious multitudes <note>A reference to the fall of Lucifer and the rebel angels.</note>. 

However, one may say with reverence regarding the Heavenly Hierarchy that when God reveals things previously unknown, it serves as a "purification" for the lower angels. It leads them to a more perfect knowledge of the communications that come from God. It "purifies" them from their ignorance regarding things they did not yet understand perfectly. They are guided through the highest and most divine beings toward the brighter splendors of the visions of God. 

Thus, there are ranks being illuminated, initiated, and purified, as well as ranks that purify, illuminate, and perfect, following the example of the Heavenly Hierarchy. The highest and most divine minds purify the subordinate, holy ranks from all ignorance (according to their specific rank and proportion). They equip them with the most divine illuminations and perfections in the pure science of the highest divine concepts. As we have already said, and as the scriptures <note>original: "oracles."</note> divinely demonstrate, not all the heavenly orders are equal in their sacred knowledge of the light that comes from God. Instead, the highest ranks are illuminated directly by God. Then, through them—though still ultimately from God—the lower ranks are illuminated with the brilliant glory of the divine rays, according to their own capacity.

## CHAPTER VII <note>original: "CAPUT VII"</note>

### *Concerning the Rites celebrated over those who have died <note>original: "fallen asleep"</note>.*

Having defined these things, I think it is necessary to describe the religious rites performed for those who have died. For here, too, there is a difference between the sacred and the profane. Just as their ways of life are different, they also differ when approaching death. Those who have lived a religious life look toward the unfailing promises of God. Because they have seen proof of these promises in the resurrection he has proclaimed, they reach the goal of death with a firm and unwavering hope. They do so in godly rejoicing, as if they are finishing holy athletic contests—knowing that their rewards will be found in a perfect and endless life and salvation through their future total resurrection. <note>The author notes the sequence: the soul is perfected first, and the body follows later.</note> For holy souls, which might potentially change for the worse during this present life, will undergo a most godlike transition to an unchangeable <note>See 1 John 3:2.</note> condition during the <term>regeneration</term> <note>This refers to the resurrection and the renewal of the world.</note>. 

Now, the pure bodies of these holy souls were joined with them as partners, companions, and fellow-combatants in their divine struggles. Because they remained steadfast throughout a life lived for God, these bodies will also receive their own resurrection. <note>See 1 Corinthians 15:52.</note> For, having been made one with the holy souls to which they were united in this life—having become members <note>See 1 Corinthians 6:15.</note> of Christ—they will receive godlike and imperishable immortality and a blessed rest. <note>See Hebrews 4:11.</note> In this respect, then, the "sleep" of the righteous is full of comfort and unshaken hope as it draws near the goal of the divine struggles. <note>See 2 Timothy 4:6-8.</note> 

However, among the profane, some <note>Plato, *Phaedo* 1.54.</note> illogically think they are heading toward non-existence. Others <note>Plato, *Phaedo* 1.62-3.</note> believe that the bond linking the body to its own soul will be severed once and for all. They do not think a divine life and blessed rest are suitable for the body, because they have not been sufficiently instructed in the knowledge of divine things, which teaches that our most godlike life in Christ has already begun. <note>See Colossians 3:3-4.</note> 

But others <note>Plato, *Phaedo* 1.64.</note> assign souls to unions with other, different bodies. In doing so, I believe they commit an injustice: after a body has labored together with a godly soul, they unfairly deprive that body of its rightful reward once it has reached the goal of the divine course. 

Still others <note>See Matthew 22:28.</note>—and I do not know how they have wandered into such earthly ways of thinking—say that the most holy and blessed inheritance promised to the devout is similar to our life in this world. They wrongly reject the idea that those who are equal to the angels will have nourishment appropriate for a different kind of life. But no one would ever

The most holy men do not abandon their hope for such foolish reasons. Knowing that their whole selves will receive the Christlike inheritance once they have reached the goal of this present life, they see the road to immortality <note>original: "incorruption"</note> drawing nearer. They praise the gifts of the supreme Divinity <note>original: "Godhead"</note> and are filled with a divine satisfaction. They no longer fear falling into a worse condition, for they well know that they will firmly and everlastingly hold the good things they have already acquired. 

But those who are full of sins and stains—even if they received some religious instruction yet chose to reject it from their minds to their own destruction, following ruinous desires—view things differently as they approach the end of their lives. The divine regulation of the scriptures will no longer appear to them as a subject of scorn,¹ as it did before. Instead, when they look with new eyes upon the ruined pleasures of their passions and realize the holy life they thoughtlessly abandoned, they are piteously separated from this life against their will. Because of their shameful lives, they are led away toward no blessed hope.² 

While none of these sinners attain the rest of the holy, the holy man who reaches the end of his struggles is filled with a sacred consolation. With great satisfaction, he draws near to the path of holy rebirth <note>original: "regeneration"</note>. The close friends of the one who has died <note>original: "fallen asleep"</note>, as befits their divine fellowship, pronounce him blessed for reaching an end crowned with victory in answer to his prayers. They offer up songs of thanksgiving to the Author of victory, praying that they themselves may reach the same inheritance. Then they take him and bring him to the Hierarch <term>Hierarch: A high-ranking religious leader, such as a bishop.</term>, as if presenting him for a holy crown of victory. The Hierarch gladly receives him and performs the rites established by holy men for those who have died in the faith.

The Divine Hierarch gathers the holy choir. If the person who has died held a priestly <note>original: "sacerdotal"</note> rank, the Hierarch places him low before the Holy Altar and offers prayer and thanksgiving to God. But if he belonged to the rank of the holy monks, he is placed near the hallowed sanctuary, before the entrance used by the priests. Then the Hierarch finishes the prayer of thanksgiving to God. Next, the ministers <term>Leitourgoi: Assistants or deacons who serve during the liturgy.</term> read the faithful promises concerning our resurrection contained in the Holy Scriptures <note>original: "Divine Lections"</note>, and they chant songs from the Book of Psalms⁴ that share the same teaching and power. First, the deacon dismisses the catechumens <note>students receiving religious instruction before baptism</note> and calls aloud the names of the holy people who have already died <note>original: "fallen asleep"</note>. Among these names, he includes the person who has just ended his life, deeming him worthy of being registered in the same rank. The deacon then urges everyone to seek a blessed end in Christ. Next, the Divine Hierarch <note>The high priest or bishop</note> comes forward and offers a most holy prayer over the deceased; after the prayer, he kisses the deceased, followed by everyone else who is present. When everyone has shared this greeting, the Hierarch pours oil upon the deceased. After offering a prayer for everyone, he places the body in a grave befitting his status, alongside other holy bodies of the same rank.

### III.—*Contemplation* <note>The spiritual or symbolic interpretation of the ritual</note>

Now, if the uninitiated <note>original: "profane"</note> should see or hear these rites being celebrated by us, they will, I suppose, burst with laughter and pity us for our folly. But we should not wonder at this. For as the Scripture says, "Unless you believe, you shall not understand."¹ Now, let those of us who have contemplated the spiritual meaning of these rites say this: while Jesus leads us to the light, it is not without reason that the Hierarch conducts the deceased to a place among those of the same rank. 

This shows spiritually that, in the resurrection <note>original: "regeneration"</note>, everyone will receive the inheritance they chose for themselves during their life here. For example, if someone lived a God-like and most holy life (as far as a human can imitate God), he will live for all ages to come in a divine and blessed state. But if he led a life less perfect than the divine likeness, yet still a holy life, he will receive holy rewards of that same kind. The Hierarch, having given thanks for this divine righteousness, offers a sacred prayer. He celebrates the worshipful Deity for defeating the unjust and tyrannical power that works against us all, and for leading us toward our own most just judgments.

The chants and readings of the divine promises explain the most blessed inheritances that will be given to those who have attained divine perfection. These inheritances awaiting the deceased stimulate those who are still living to strive for a similar perfection. 

However, notice that not all classes of those undergoing purification are dismissed from the ceremony. Only the catechumens are put out of the holy place. This class is entirely uninstructed in every holy initiation and is not permitted to view any religious celebrations, large or small. They have not yet received the faculty of contemplating the holy mysteries through the "Birth from God" <note>Baptism</note>, which is the source and gift of light. Other classes of those being purified have already been instructed in sacred tradition; but, as they have—

For those who have foolishly returned to an evil way of life—since it is necessary for them to complete their training toward better things—they are rightly excluded from Divine visions and Communions <note>The Sacraments, particularly the Eucharist</note>, even those performed under symbols. This is because they would be harmed by participating in them in an unholy manner and would grow to further despise both the Divine Mysteries and themselves. However, it is not inappropriate for them to be present at the ceremonies now being celebrated. Here, they are clearly taught and see for themselves the uncertainty of death among us, the glories of the saints chanted from the unfailing oracles <note>The Scriptures</note>, and the reminder that the sufferings reserved for unholy people like themselves will be without end. 

It may perhaps be profitable for them to see a person who has religiously finished his life’s journey being reverently honored by the public declaration of the **Ministers** <note>original: *Leitourgoi*—those who perform public religious service</note>, as he is numbered among the saints forever.¹ And, perhaps, they too will desire to reach a similar state and will learn, through the instruction of the Deacons, that the final completion <note>original: "consummation"</note> in Christ is truly blessed. 

Then the Hierarch <note>The High Priest or Bishop</note>, coming forward, offers a sacred prayer over the deceased. After the prayer, the Hierarch himself salutes the body, followed by everyone else who is present. As for the prayer itself, it beseeches the supreme Divine Goodness to forgive the deceased for all the failings committed through human weakness and to place him in the light² and land of the living³; in the "bosom of Abraham,⁴ Isaac, and Jacob"; in a place where grief, sorrow, and sighing are no more. 

It is evident, I think, that these rewards of the holy are most blessed. For what can be compared to an immortality entirely without grief and shining with light? This is especially true if all the promises which surpass human understanding—which are shown to us through signs adapted to our limited capacity—actually fall short in their description of the true reality. For we must remember the truth of the saying: "Eye has not seen, nor ear heard, nor has it entered into the heart of man to conceive, the things God has prepared for those who love⁵ him." The "bosoms" of the blessed Patriarchs and of the other holy men are, in my judgment, the most divine and blessed inheritance which awaits all godly people in that final completion⁶ which never grows old and is full of happiness.

But you may, perhaps, say that while these things are correctly stated by us, you want to know for what reason the Hierarch prays to the supreme Divine Goodness, asking for the forgiveness of the faults committed by the deceased and for his admission into the brightest inheritance among godly men of the same rank. For, if everyone shall receive, by the judgment of God, the equivalent of what he has done in this present life—whether it be good or otherwise—and the deceased has already finished his actions within this life, how can any prayer offered by the...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the efficacy of prayer for the deceased, arguing that divine justice assigns each person an inheritance based on their life's actions, and that even the prayers of holy men cannot change a person's fate if they are unworthy.</meta>

<page-num>93</page-num>

### ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY.

Will the Hierarch <note>The High Priest or Bishop</note> be able to transfer the deceased to a different destiny <note>original: "lot"</note> other than the one they earned, as if it were a reward for their life here? I know well, following the Oracles <note>The Holy Scriptures</note>, that each person will receive an inheritance that matches their life. For the Lord has already decided concerning each individual: everyone "shall receive the things done in his body according to what he has done, whether it be good or bad."<gloss>1</gloss> Indeed, the reliable tradition of the Scriptures teaches us that the prayers of the righteous are effective only for those who are worthy of such pious prayers during this present life, and much less <gloss>2 <note>original: "μήτιγε μετα θάνατον" — "much less after death"</note></gloss> after death. Did the prayers of Samuel gain anything for Saul?<gloss>3</gloss> Or did the intercession of the Prophet <note>Jeremiah</note><gloss>4</gloss> bring any benefit to the people of Israel? 

It is as if the sun were shining its full brilliance upon healthy eyes, yet someone tried to enjoy that midday light while deliberately blinding their own vision. Such a person clings to impossible and wild expectations. In the same way, someone who begs for the prayers of holy men, but neglects the holy efforts required of himself and ignores the most radiant and beneficial commandments, loses out on Divine gifts through his own carelessness.

Nevertheless, I affirm—on the authority of the Scriptures—that the prayers of the righteous <gloss>5</gloss> are beneficial in every way during this life, in the following manner. If someone longs for holy gifts and has a religious heart ready to receive them, but approaches a godly man because they are aware of their own inadequacy, and if they persuade that man to become their helper and fellow-suppliant, they will benefit greatly. In fact, they will receive a benefit superior to all others. They will attain the Divine gifts they pray for because the supreme Divine Goodness assists them. This is due to their own pious self-knowledge, their reverence for holy men, their sincere craving for the religious gifts they requested, and their brotherly, God-like disposition. For it has been firmly established by Divine decree that God's gifts are given in a most orderly way: they are granted to those who are fit to receive them, through those who are fit to distribute them.

If anyone should despise this sacred arrangement and give in to a wretched pride, deeming himself sufficient for Divine communication <note>original: "Converse"</note> without others, and looking down upon godly men; and if he should further make requests that are unworthy of God and not holy; and if his heart is not in harmony with the Divine will, then he will fail in his foolish request through his own fault. 

Now, regarding the prayer already mentioned, which the Hierarch offers over the deceased...

<meta>This page explains the role of the Hierarch as an interpreter of God's judgment, detailing why he prays for the pious dead and how his authority to forgive or retain sins reflects the divine will rather than his own personal impulse.</meta>

<page-num>94</page-num>

# ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY.

...the deceased <note>original: "defunct"</note>, we think it necessary to mention the tradition which has come down to us from our inspired leaders. The Divine Hierarch <note>The High Priest or Bishop</note>, as the scriptures <note>original: "oracles"</note> say, is the interpreter of the supreme divine judgments. For he is the messenger<gloss>1</gloss> of the Lord God Almighty. He has learned, then, from the divinely transmitted scriptures, that for those who have lived their life in piety, a most radiant and divine<gloss>2</gloss> life is the reward given according to their merits, by the most just standards. At the same time, the Divine Love for humanity overlooks, through its goodness, the flaws that have come to them through human weakness (since no one, as the scriptures say, is pure and without stain).<gloss>3</gloss>

Now, the Hierarch knew these things to have been promised by the reliable scriptures. He asks, then, that these things may come to pass, and that the righteous rewards may be given to those who have lived piously. At the same time, because he is shaped as an image of goodness in imitation of the Divine, he prays for gifts for others as if they were favors to himself. But, at the same time, he knows that the promises will certainly be fulfilled, and he clearly demonstrates to those present that the things he asks for according to Divine law will be entirely realized for those who have been perfected in a life of God. For the Hierarch, the interpreter of supreme Divine Justice, would never seek things that are not most pleasing to the Most High God, or that were not divinely promised to be given by Him. 

Therefore, he does not offer these prayers over the wicked who have died; not only because in doing so he would turn away from his office as interpreter and would arrogantly claim, on his own authority, a function of the Hierarchy without being moved by the Divine Lawgiver, but also because he would fail to obtain his improper <note>original: "abominable"</note> request. He would, quite naturally, hear the Divine Scripture say of himself: “You ask, and do not receive, because you ask with wrong motives.”<gloss>4</gloss> Therefore, the Divine Hierarch prays for things divinely promised and dear to God, which will certainly be granted in every respect. He demonstrates his own likeness to the good, loving God, and declares, as the interpreter of God, the gifts which will be received by the devout. Thus, the Hierarchs have powers of discernment as interpreters of the Divine Judgments. It is not as though the All-Wise Deity—to put it mildly—were submissively following their unreasonable impulses, but rather that they, as interpreters of God, are deciding by the prompting of the supreme judging Spirit which people have already been judged by God according to their merits. For “Receive,” he says, the Holy Spirit; “whose<gloss>5</gloss> sins you forgive, they are forgiven; whose you retain, they..."
